From Hibs casual to a sold-out Hydro - the incredible story of bare-knuckle boxing's Leith Warrior

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The hands might tell a story although Lewis Knox will hope they know when to stop blabbing. After all, he doesn't want his tools of the trade to betray him.

These fists helped him survive on Leith’s mean streets. As a former Hibs casual, he once used them to defend his club’s honour.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

In a more legitimate context, one less likely to interest the authorities, they are crucial for his current job installing sofas up and down the country. And more pressingly, in Glasgow on Saturday, these hands will be used as weapons with the aim of drawing blood.

“All legal and above board,” Knox clarifies. Welcome to the world of “BKFC”, which stands for Bare Knuckle Fighting Championship. The promotion, co-owned by controversial UFC star Conor McGregor, hits Scotland for the first time this weekend.

Only the All Blacks’ visit to Murrayfield, Celtic v Kilmarnock and possibly Aberdeen v Hibs will attract a bigger crowd in a sporting context.

Still, it’s guaranteed none of those involved in these fixtures will have spent the start of this week lugging sofas up and down stairwells. “I install them into houses from Berwick-Upon-Tweed to near enough Stonehaven," says Knox. "12-hour shifts, 7 am to 7 pm. Four days on, four days off. But I am still training every night. As well as being a dad of three."

Even the All Blacks, who have been utilising the gym where Knox has been training under the watchful eye of Ali Thomson, might wince at the thought of this schedule. Thomson describes Knox as a "phenomenon". He complains his own arms are aching from doing pads with the focused fighter in the run-up to this weekend.

"People say things are impossible,” ponders Knox. “But you know if you really want it? Things can be done. My mum, my dad, my family. They all support me. Everyone is tuned into it. They all try to help out. This is not an overnight thing, this has been three years in the making to get to where I am now. I have worked so hard."

He was in Fife as recently as Monday delivering sofas but who knows where he will be this time next year? The bare-knuckle scene is taking off, boosted by McGregor's involvement. "I'd like to turn pro," says Knox, 29. Remember that Hearts fan ridiculed for claiming on camera that he’d be willing to have his hands chopped off if it meant his team winning the league?

Knox loves Hibs. He was thrilled by last weekend’s League Cup semi-final win over Hearts at Hampden, which he watched on TV because he was deep in fight camp. However, he can’t afford to strike any bargain involving the prospect of losing his hands. As a bare-knuckle boxer on the cusp of his biggest ever fight, they are, after his three children, among the most precious things on earth to him.

Perhaps a little Atrixo might be in order. They are battered, bruised and a little disfigured. Hardly surprising really, since they are used to pound his opponents. “Bone on bone,” he says. “There is nothing more raw.” He reports that he hasn't broken his hands in his last three fights. "They are solid, like sledgehammers," he says.

Rather than slathering them with hand cream, Knox is considering the old fighters’ trick of sticking his hands into a bucket of sand – or rice. “It strengthens up the skin,” he says.

He broke two knuckles in his very first (official) bare-knuckle fight three years ago this month. Slightly horrifyingly, these knuckles now appear to have disappeared completely. “Yeah, disintegrated,” he says. “Two knuckles have gone, one has dipped (into the hand). As for my pinkie, I can’t stretch it out."

Knox demonstrates this inability to extend his little finger in the café where we are sitting. The Leith Warrior, as he’s known, has ordered a flat white with a shot of caramel (there was still plenty of time to make the required fighting weight of 66 kgs). If not for the damage to his hands, he seems incredibly unwarrior-like.

Softly spoken and trim, almost delicate-looking, the tattoos extending past his collar line on his neck are perhaps the main evidence of a life lived on the edge.

“I always wanted tattoos,” he says. As one suspects is the case with many people, he first succumbed to the needle and ink on holiday in Magaluf.

“A star on my chest and a Hibs tattoo on the back of my calf,” he says. “I thought I had to get serious ones after that. Ones devoted to my gran and many grandad, who both played huge parts in my life from a young age. I also have a gladiator on my arm and neck tattoos…It has become part of the job!”

As for the colours he sports when competing in the circular four-rope ring, which is designed to encourage fast-paced bouts, it wasn’t up for discussion. “Green and white, I always represent where I am from,” he says. “And a Hibs badge on my shorts. I am never not representing Leith.

“I grew up in Leith, a lot of gang culture, an environment of violence as a young boy,” he continues. “You had to be streetwise from a young age I suppose. Leith has its good and bad sides. For me, it’s one of the best places in the world. But it can be one of the worst places in the world.

"Knife crime, drugs. It’s a mix of cultures, backgrounds. I grew up in all of that. But I was never ultra-violent.”

His mother, Niki, once ran the Junction bar. Knox enjoys an obvious status in the area. A taxi driver beeps his horn as soon as he steps out of the door of our meeting place on Easter Road. Cross Knoxy and you cross Leith.

He knows people, Irvine Welsh included. The author – a more than decent boxer himself - has been mentoring Knox before his featherweight division fight against Tommy “Chaos” Carus from Liverpool at the OVO Hydro on Saturday night. Welsh has already featured in an Instagram video promoting Knox’s fight, playing the role of his manager.

“Oi, Knoxy!” he calls out after taking a phone call. “Get your dancing shoes on! We’ve got ourselves a pagger.”

Knox v Carus, or the Leith Warrior v Chaos, or indeed Scotland v England, is the third fight of 11 on a bill topped by Sean Weir’s light heavyweight title bout with Leigh Cohoon, a late stand-in for Irishman Conor ‘Da Crook’ Cooke.

Cooke’s team cited concerns about the venue layout and ticketing arrangements for their withdrawal. Some have interpreted this as another way of saying they were worried sectarian/football-related hooliganism – Weir was once a member of the Rangers Riot Crew casuals group - could flare.

Almost 11,000 are expected to converge on the Hydro for the event. BKFC is dedicated to “fighter safety”. According to their official bumph, the organisation prides itself in being “the truest form of bare-knuckle fighting”. Fighters are not allowed to wrap their hands to within one inch of the knuckle. This weekend’s spectacle is the first legally sanctioned and regulated bare knuckle event in Scotland.

For the uninitiated, the fights last for five two-minute rounds. If there isn’t a knockout, it tends to be fairly obvious who’s won.

“Cuts,” says Knox. “It comes down to facial damage. Because in boxing or any gloved fight, you can punch them ten times with a glove and not make a mark, you can punch them once with a bare knuckle and split someone’s head open.

“It is about getting as much of that in in a short period of time. The first punch usually sets the tone for the fight. It depends on where you catch him, I suppose. It is all about just getting in there and being horrible.”

It’s easy to question the ethics of unpadded pugilism. However, defenders of this ancient and previously underground practice insist it’s a safer, more honest form of boxing. "It is better for the brain, much better," claims Knox. "In boxing you get the double shock with the glove – boom, boom. It rattles the brain. You don’t see the damage facially necessarily. With bare knuckle, bone is hitting bone. That’s it. The facial damage will be shown. Internally your brain is not getting rattled for 12 rounds.

“It is five rounds of two minutes. If you last that, although you might get a few cuts, your brain is fine. To me, my brain feels fine! In my 50s and 60s I will still have a functional brain. I might have a few scars and a few missing teeth but I move pretty well (to avoid the punches). My nose is still straight...”

Inside the ring might be a veritable haven as the evening hots up on Saturday. “It is like any combat sport,” says Knox. “The crowd can be unpredictable. A bit like a football crowd. I just hope that because this event is so big for Scotland and so big for the bare-knuckle scene and for lads like myself that nothing serious kicks off. There will be some ‘characters’ there, you always get the odd person. You get that anywhere.”

Knox anticipates an unholy commotion when the first bars of Born Slippy strike up. The Underworld track is his walk on song, unsurprisingly given the Trainspotting connotations. Sunshine on Leith just wouldn’t do it in this context. Fine for after last weekend’s win for Hibs at Hampden against Hearts, but perhaps a little too plaintive, a little too pretty, for a night of good-to-honest testosterone-fuelled violence.

As much as the Easter Road club might wish otherwise, a line – if admittedly indirect - can be drawn between Hibs and Knox’s prowess in the ring.

“Before I got into bare knuckle, I boxed," he says. "But I was also fighting in football as well. I grew up in a place where there was a lot of gang culture, an environment of violence as a young boy. You had to be streetwise from a young age I suppose.”

He ran with the Capital City Service – Hibs casuals – from 17-years-old onwards. “I found it attractive,” he says. “It was something I felt I was missing: being part of a brotherhood, a group.”

The casuals scene, including rival groups such as ASC (Aberdeen Soccer Casuals) and Dundee Utility, seems to belong to another era of Scottish football. But the fights still happen, just further away from stadiums.

“Anywhere there isn’t cameras,” he says. “Back in the 80s and most of the 90s, you had groups of a hundred plus. Since TV took over, groups became smaller, more confined, more secret. You had to move with the times I suppose. You couldn’t wander around in groups of hundreds, you’d be seen by all the cameras.”

It became violence by appointment. “You’d phone and say: ‘Meet you there’. A set meet. Sometimes nothing would ever happen. You’d phone all day and nothing would happen. But you’d still have that day out with your mates.”

The arrangements could be painstaking. “It was all planned out. You needed management skills! A lot went into it. You didn’t just turn up and fight. What time were we fighting, what time were the trains, what pubs to go to? It was a lot of work. I could probably pass a management course now quite easily!”

Unlike some, Knox would always endeavour to go the game. He was and remains a football fan and played at a decent level. His first ambition was to be a professional footballer. He played for Hawick Royal Albert and Coldstream in the East of Scotland league – on the wing, wing-back or full-back and then, latterly, as a sweeper with a reputation for never shirking a tackle.

“I was still involved in the casuals scene, still attracted to it,” he says. “It was not all about violence. It was about being with your mates. We dressed the same, liked the same music. Away days having a laugh. It wasn’t all about fighting.”

But some of it undoubtedly was. He remembers once playing in a Scottish Junior Cup tie for Newcraighall, which had kicked off early. “I then went home and got changed before a fight with Raith Rovers, who were playing a Scottish Cup tie against Hibs,” he says.

“Everyone has got a bit older now. The scene has changed. Six guys v six guys, in a forest somewhere. Last team standing wins. To me that’s not football casuals, because you are away from everything.”

“I am not saying football violence is good,” he says. “But there’s so much more to it. It’s guys looking forward to being with their mates, they maybe need that at the end of the week. It’s a release. It acts as therapy for some people.”

The message is: don’t diss what you’ve never tried. The same could apply to bare-knuckle boxing, which is now edging into the mainstream to the extent that this weekend’s extravaganza has been advertised on the side of buses.

Maybe an invite onto the pitch at Hibs before a game might be forthcoming for Knox – it ought to be if he prevails this weekend, which would mean he is the No. 1 contender for the UK featherweight bare-knuckle title.

His ultimate dream is one likely to give Edinburgh city fathers pause. “My goal, my dream fight, if it wasn't Easter Road, would be on the castle esplanade,” he says. “Outdoors, in the summer. It would have a gladiatorial feel about it, you know? That would just be amazing.”

New museum dedicated to Italian Modernist Mario Sironi must not become ‘endorsement of fascism’, art historians warn

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The museum is expected to be housed in the National Association of War Disabled and Wounded Veterans, a monumental fascist era building inaugurated in 1928

Courtesy of the Italian ministry of culture

Plans by Giorgia Meloni’s government to open a new museum in Rome dedicated to Mario Sironi, the Modernist painter who was a supporter of Benito Mussolini, risk sanitising Italy's fascist past, two leading historians have warned.

The state-run museum is expected to open next year within the Associazione Nazionale fra Mutilati ed Invalidi di Guerra (National Association of War Disabled and Wounded Veterans), a monumental fascist era building inaugurated in 1928. The building is home to two major frescoes by Sironi painted ten years later. One depicts Mussolini on horseback.

The museum will display 30 works recently acquired by Italy's culture ministry from the Mario Sironi Archive in Rome, including paintings, drawings and preparatory sketches. A further 90 works will be loaned by the archive.

Unveiling the plans on Tuesday, Alessandro Giuli, the culture minister, said the project was “the defining achievement of my term in office, and the one of which I am most proud”.

Meloni's Brothers of Italy has political roots in the postwar Italian neo-fascist movement. Press materials for the museum do not mention fascism or Mussolini.

Sironi joined the fascist movement in 1919, contributing more than 1,700 political cartoons and illustrations to Il Popolo d’Italia, Mussolini’s newspaper. The museum will feature works spanning more than four decades from the 1910s to the 1950s, covering distinct phases from Sironi's artistic development including Futurism, metaphysical-influenced works and monumental commissions awarded under the fascist regime.

Courtesy of the Italian ministry of culture

Members of Italy’s cultural sector have emphasised the need for the institution to address Seroni’s politics.

Tomaso Montanari, an art historian and rector of the Università per Stranieri di Siena (University for Foreigners of Siena), said that he did not oppose the museum but insisted it must explicitly distance itself from fascism.

“Since Italy is a republic founded on anti-fascism, the museum's educational material must make clear that there is no endorsement of fascism," Montanari told The Art Newspaper.

Montanari, who sits on the culture ministry's scientific committee, said that the initial project for the museum focussed “overwhelmingly” on Sironi's fascist period work. He added that the ministry "partially" heeded his argument for works from other periods to be included.

Mario Sironi, Autoritratto (Self-portrait)

Courtesy of the Italian ministry of culture

The art historian and curator Fabio Benzi, who is on the Sironi museum's scientific committee, told the French-language publication Le Journal Des Arts: "The danger is of legitimising a certain authoritarian aesthetic under the guise of cultural rediscovery".

The culture ministry has been contacted for comment.

Last year, the ministry announced it had acquired the former Rome home of Giacomo Balla, the futurist painter who initially supported fascism, which operates as a museum.

On Thursday [5 November], key members of Meloni's government presented a new exhibition celebrating the 90th anniversary of the EUR district in Rome, the city's grandest example of fascist-era planning.

The inverted photo of the Italian prime minister has drawn comparisons to images of Benito Mussolini's execution

Alarmed post-colonial researchers have raised concerns over plans to open Museo Italo Africano "Ilaria Alpi" in late 2021

Curators of the controversial Salò institution say museum will “neither demonise nor defend” fascist leader Benito Mussolini

Solana's Skyline Crypto Ad Triggers Central Bank Investigation in China

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

A joint operation of the People's Bank of China with Shanghai police clamped down on the Solana Accelerate China 2026 four-day tour. Authorities blocked the project's events in Shanghai, while the financial regulator in parallel launched an investigation against those locals who helped the blockchain platform with logistics and public relations.

What drew such a harsh reaction from the Chinese authorities was 'too aggressive' offline marketing of the tour. On the evening of October 14, a team associated with Solana, together with Shanghai contractors, put giant vibrant logos of the SOL token on the Citigroup Tower skyscraper facade in Lujiazui - the very heart of Shanghai's financial district. This was not just an ad: top managers, including Solana Foundation president Lily Lu, started publicly boasting about the opening of Shanghai's new office in a prestigious building.

All this massive campaign predictably caused an excitement in crypto circles. Even Binance founder Changpeng Zhao, known simply as CZ, expressed his amusement, calling 'amazing' the fact that Solana managed to not only do a promo in China, but get an office there as well. BNB Chain did not achieve such a success, added CZ.

It seems like the organizers tried to bypass the regulatory barriers using a positioning trick. The four-day tour between Shanghai, Hangzhou, Shenzhen, and Beijing was marketed strictly around technologies like AI agents, robots, IT development - not about crypto at all.

The maneuver did not get recognition in China to say the least, and the backlash did not take long to follow.

The morning session on October 16 in Shangri-La was shut down outright, with participants held for over an hour on the street after police suddenly arrived. Lily Lu tried to narrow the angles and stated that the cancellation was caused by registrations quadrupling, so the site was not ready to invite all registrants.

Fast forward to November 6 and representatives of the People's Bank of China, in an exclusive interview with the state agency Financial News, finally ended speculations on what actually happened. He directly called the skyline ad and all the aligned events an illegal marketing of virtual currencies. The Central Bank's position is 'black and white' there: SOL is a cryptocurrency and Solana's infra not only facilitates coins issuance, but also helps stablecoin turnover (especially USDT), which is banned.

Organizers in the face of Solana Foundation breached the crypto-risk agreement jointly issued back in February 2026 by eight Chinese authority departments. The document directly bans all the promotion of overseas crypto projects and implements individual liability for Chinese citizens and companies that help to do this.

Solana Foundation hasn't provided any official comments on the matter yet.

The signal by People's Bank of China is unambiguous though: full cleanup of all the mainland market out of foreign blockchain networks and activities associated with them.

Premier League player stopped by armed police for carrying bow and arrows in public

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A Premier League soccer player was stopped by police in England after being spotted carrying a bow and arrows near a reservoir, prompting a response reportedly involving six police cars.

Amar Dedic, a 24-year-old defender for Newcastle United, was with a friend in Northumberland, England, when a member of the public spotted them carrying the equipment and alerted authorities.

According to reports, six police vehicles were dispatched to the area, including armed officers, who eventually stopped the pair in a car around seven miles from where they were first seen.

Police searched the vehicle and confiscated the bow and arrows, but determined that neither man posed a threat to the public.

In a statement, Northumbria Police confirmed that officers had responded to reports of two men carrying a bow and arrows while walking near a reservoir in the Harlow Hill area.

“Officers attended and subsequently stopped a vehicle in the Darras Hall area and seized a bow and arrows,” police said.

“At this stage, it is understood they were for recreational use and there was no threat to the public.”

Police said their enquiries were ongoing, although they did not publicly identify Dedic in their statement.

BBC Sport reported that the soccer player had no malicious intentions and fully cooperated with officers throughout the incident.

By chance, two other professional soccer players, Newcastle goalkeeper Mark Gillespie and former defender Paul Dummett, reportedly arrived while police were speaking to Dedic.

Dedic joined Newcastle United in August 2026 for a reported transfer fee of around £30 million ($39 million).

The Bosnia-Herzegovina international has made 10 appearances for the club across league and cup competitions since his arrival. Newcastle United has not publicly commented on the incident.

First Portugal call-up for Monza’s Varela alongside Mora, Ramos and Tavares

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In-form Monza striker Gustavo Varela has earned his first call-up to the Portugal national team for November’s Nations League fixtures, along with Roma teenager Rodrigo Mora, Milan striker Goncalo Ramos and Lazio full-back Nuno Tavares.

Portugal will be in Nations League action against Denmark on Saturday, November 14 and away against Wales on Tuesday, November 17 and will have four Serie A stars to call upon.

There has been a first call-up for 21-year-old Monza striker Varela, who leads the Serie A goalscoring charts with nine goals from his first nine league appearances of the 2026-27 campaign. With cup fixtures added in, he is now up to 11 goals from his first 10 competitive Monza appearances.

19-year-old Rodrigo Mora has also taken well to life in Italy and has provided three goals from his first eight appearances in Serie A since joining from Porto during the summer transfer window. He could be set to earn his first caps for his nation this break, although this is not his first international call-up.

Goncalo Ramos became Milan’s all-time most expensive signing when he joined from Paris Saint-Germain over the summer and has kept his place in the Portugal national team squad for November. He has scored three goals and provided one assist from his first 13 appearances with the Rossoneri.

There is also room for Nuno Tavares, who has started the last nine Serie A matches in a row. His Lazio side sit at the top of the Serie A table with 28 points from a possible 30 after the first 10 league matches of the season.

Goalkeepers: Diogo Costa (Porto), Rui Silva (Sporting CP), Samuel Soares (Benfica).

Defenders: Diogo Dalot (Manchester United), Alberto Costa (Porto), Ruben Dias (Manchester City), Tomas Araujo (Benfica), Renato Veiga (Villarreal), Antonio Silva (Bournemouth), Joao Cancelo (Barcelona), Nuno Tavares (Lazio).

Midfielders: Joao Palhinha (Benfica), Ruben Neves (Al-Hilal), Mateus Fernandes (Tottenham), Vitinha (PSG), Joao Neves (PSG), Bernardo Silva (Real Madrid).

Forwards: Pedro Neto (Chelsea), Francisco Trincao (Al-Ahli), Carlos Forbs (Club Brugge), Rafael Leao (Galatasaray), Geovany Quenda (Chelsea), Bruno Fernandes (Manchester United), Joao Felix (Al-Nassr), Rodrigo Mora (Roma), Goncalo Ramos (Milan), Fabio Silva (Borussia Dortmund), Gustavo Varela (Monza).

The Skull Mask, the Spooky Mask, the Bunny Hood, and more are part of the Happy Mask Shop quest in OOT Remake

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Happy Mask Shop in Zelda Ocarina of Time has you run around Hyrule trading masks with characters for Rupees, including the Skull Mask, Spooky Mask, Bunny Hood, and the Keaton mask. Though these masks only serve as commodities to be traded with specific characters, if you can allocate all four to the right characters and complete this OOT quest, the Happy Mask Shop will give you access to four new masks that all have a very specific utility. Below I'll show you how to hand out all the Masks and the specific locations you need to look.

Keep in mind that the Switch 2 version is the same as the original, in that the Happy Mask Shop won't open in The Legend of Zelda Ocarina of Time until you open the passage to the Goron City. Fortunately, the guard who opens the passage will actually tell you that the Mask Shop is now open, flagging that you can start this questline.

Bought from the Mask Shop for 10 Rupees, you need to give this to the Guard in Kakariko Village who opens the passage for you, the same one who lets you know that the Mask Shop is open. He'll give you 15 Rupees in return.

Bought for 20 Rupees, head back to the starting area of Kokiri Village, where you start off and get the first Shield and Sword in Zelda Ocarina of Time, and turn North into the Lost Woods.

The moment you enter, head left through the hollow trunk to meet The Skull Kid, playing a tune on top of a log. He, understandably, will offer to take the Skull Mask from you, though only pay you ten for it. Cheapskate.

After buying the Spooky Mask for 30 Rupees, head back to Kakariko Village, and this time take the East exit to the Graveyard, but do so during daytime. Here you'll find a boy wandering around. Speak to him to give him the Spooky Mask, for which he'll give you 30 Rupees back.

The final mask in this sequence is bought for 50 Rupees. You can only sell it once you've progressed to the point where you have obtained the Three Spiritual Stones in the main story.

Sign up to our Switch 2 Spotlight newsletter to make sure you get the very latest news on Nintendo's new console and its games every week

Once this happens, head to Hyrule Field. There'll be a man who runs in circles around the field, practicing his running. If you go during the night, he'll be sitting beside the road and you won't have to keep pace. Either way, if you give him the Bunny Hood, he will entirely fill your wallet with Rupees, so it's a good idea to have expanded it as much as possible prior to the trade to really capitalize on that.

Having given these masks out, the Happy Mask Shop will now have four new masks in store that you can get, all of which serve a particular function. Here's what they do:

It's really only the Mask of Truth that provides the major advantage to gameplay, though some of the dialogue options and reactions from NPCs can prompt interesting lore information. Likewise, the cash infusion delivered by the Bunny Hood NPC can make Link incredibly rich, a great reward in and of itself.

Google's Find Hub can finally notify you if you've left a tracker behind

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The company has finally introduced a ‘Notify when left behind’ notification for tracking devices

Google has started rolling out a feature for the Find Hub that will send you a notification if you've left a location tracker — or the item you've attached it to — behind. Apple has had separation alerts for years, letting you enable them for Find My-enabled tracking devices so you can quickly get notified when they're not with you. Samsung added the feature to SmartThings for devices like its Galaxy SmartTags and Buds a few years ago. With separation alerts enabled, you may have better chances of retrieving an item you've dropped or forgotten, say, before you get too far or before you've boarded your plane.

The feature's rollout was first reported by 9to5Google. To get separation alerts on your Android phone, open the Find Hub app, choose a tag from your device list, then select the Settings gear icon. On the tracker's page, you'll see a new section where you can enable Notify when left behind for that specific device. If you switch it on, you'll get an alert from the Find Hub network if you get far away enough from the tracker, unless you've left it at your designated Home address on Google Maps.

Currently, the feature seems to be live in the US and in some parts of Europe. It also seems available across brands, with 9to5Google able to access the feature with their Moto and Chipolo trackers linked to an Android phone.

Oppo confirms its next foldable will actually know what a ‘global’ release is

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Oppo Find N6 is a stunning foldable but, for the second year in a row, it really stretches the definition of what a “global” release means. Oppo Find N7, apparently, will fix that.

Oppo Find N5 and Find N6 both saw global launches, but the company’s definition of that excluded Europe and virtually every other major market. For the most part, both foldables were essentially sold only in China, with major markets elsewhere left out. And that’s a shame, because both raised the bar for foldables in major ways.

Now, we’re looking forward to the launch of Oppo Find N7, a device rumored to be adopting the wide form factor popularized by Galaxy Z Fold 8 and iPhone Duo, and that Oppo technically offered before anyone else.

And it might actually get a global release.

Speaking to Nixanbal (via GSMArena), Oppo apparently confirmed that its next foldable will see a release in Europe. It’d be the first time an Oppo foldable made its way to the region, with the OnePlus Open serving as Oppo’s global foldable back in 2023 for its one and only generation.

As for a release date, that’s still unconfirmed, but Oppo tends to release new foldables early in the year, so March 2027, give or take, is probably when we’ll see more officially.

Denver Airport Taco Bell Worker Sues For Pay While Waiting At Security — And They May Win

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A former Taco Bell food preparer at Denver International Airport is suing the restaurant’s operator for pay for time spent going through security and getting to the restaurant before clocking in. According to Law360, the Colorado state court lawsuit also alleges that hourly employees weren’t provided paid rest breaks. I’d normally be skeptical of a claim for pay while passing through an airport checkpoint. Government-required screening looks like part of getting to work. However, Colorado law is unique and more generous here than even California. This Wouldn’t Fly In California In January, I wrote about a Delta Sky Club worker at Los Angeles International Airport seeking pay for time spent going through TSA. A similar claim by an Admirals Club employee had already lost. In Cazares v. Host International, the Ninth Circuit upheld dismissal because the employee hadn’t alleged facts showing employer control during federally mandated screening. Federal law is also favorable to employers here. The Supreme Court’s Integrity Staffing Solutions v. Busk decision held that warehouse workers didn’t have to be paid for certain employer-required security searches. Simply requiring an activity doesn’t automatically make it paid work under federal law. Colorado Specifically Includes Security Screening As Work Colorado’s wage rules list “security or safety screening”, including waiting for it, among activities that can count as paid work. The state’s labor department explains that screening required to work for an employer can qualify “whether discretionary with the employer or legally required.” Its example is federally required radiation screening for nuclear plant guards. In Rael v. Q3 Contracting in 2024, a federal judge dismissed claims involving vehicle inspections and commuting under federal law, while allowing claims for that same time under Colorado law. The Taco Bell concessionaire’s best argument is that a shared airport checkpoint remains part of the commute: it serves aviation security generally, outside the restaurant’s premises and supervision. The worker’s answer is that this is a required screening to reach the assigned workplace, exactly the sort of activity Colorado treats as work. It’s not a frivolous theory the way it was for California airport lounge employees. Interestingly, the time security to the restaurant could count as work, too. Colorado generally excludes ordinary commuting and the initial trip to a workstation. However, its travel rule includes an exception once compensable work has begun. If the security checkpoint is work, then travel from that work location to the next one can be work, too. The state’s travel time guidance even discusses walking from an entrance to a workstation after paid activity starts. I’ve written plenty about the time it takes to get around Denver airport. So this could be a lot of time. My bet is that this dispute is more likely to produce a negotiated payment than a clean employer win based on who runs the checkpoint. But that’s just going to encourage more similar suits. How To Combine Hilton Honors Points Between Accounts For Free

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Every hotel loyalty program has a different policy when it comes to being able to combine points among members. Arguably Hilton Honors has among the most generous policies of any program when it comes to letting members combine points, so I’d like to take a closer look at that in this post.

For what it’s worth, separately I’ve covered the policies of Marriott Bonvoy and World of Hyatt when it comes to combining points.

Hilton Honors offers a generous feature to allow members to combine and share points with others at no cost. First let’s go over the basics:

Technically Hilton Honors has two features by which you can combine points for free, and this can cause quite a bit of confusion. These two options are as follows:

The reality is that these two options are more similar than they may sound. The same limits exist on points transfers regardless of which method you choose:

The end result is the same, though, which is that points are being consolidated in one account. Given the similar restrictions on transfers, personally I think it’s easier to just use the points transfer feature over the points pooling feature, which just adds an unnecessary step.

Before we talk about how combining Hilton points works, why should you even care about this feature? There are a few reasons this could make a difference:

Long story short, being able to combine points just gives you a lot more flexibility.

The process of combining Hilton Honors points differs based on whether you’re just looking to make a simple transfer, or are looking to create a points pool with up to 11 accounts.

This is the most basic function, and also what I recommend. Visit the Hilton Honors points transfer page to start the process of transferring points from one Hilton Honors account to another.

You’ll be prompted to log into your Hilton Honors account, and then will be asked to enter the number of points you want to transfer (between 1,000 and 500,000), along with the name, email address, and Hilton Honors number of the person you want to transfer points to.

This is still facilitated by points.com (as points purchases usually are), except in this case you’re not paying anything. While the points are supposed to be available for redemption immediately, they may only show in your online account activity 24 hours after the transaction was processed.

This is the function to be aware of if you want to have up to 11 people combine points into one account. Visit the Hilton Honors points pooling page to start the process of creating a pooling account with multiple Hilton Honors members.

While the points transfer feature only requires the participation of the member who is transferring points, the process of pooling points requires participation of all members involved. One person can start sending invitations to pool points, which requires entering the names and email addresses of the people you want to invite.

That person will receive an email invitation to join your points pool, at which point they can transfer points to you.

The person will have to log into their Hilton Honors account, and then they’ll be brought to the same page for combining points as above.

If the member does choose to pool/transfer points, the person who invited them will be informed.

The reality is that this process is more or less the same as just combining points, with the difference being that someone is prompted to transfer points, rather than just doing so straight out of their account with no prompting.

Not only does Hilton Honors offer an excellent rate of return (in points) on hotel stays, but there are lots of other great ways to earn Hilton Honors points.

For one, there are four excellent co-branded Hilton Amex credit cards in the United States, including the following:

On top of that, Hilton Honors often sells points at a discount. In many cases there’s a 100% bonus on purchased points (or even more), which allows you to purchase points for just 0.5 cents each (or in some case even less). This can be a good deal, particularly for high-end redemptions (though Hilton Honors devaluations in recent times make these redemptions less exciting than in the past).

It’s always nice to easily be able to move points between loyalty program accounts in order to maximize them. As far as hotel loyalty programs go, Hilton Honors has what’s arguably the best option for combining points.

You can combine points at no cost, with some reasonable limits in place. You can either use the standard points transfer option, or you can go through the process of pooling points; personally I find the former option to be easier.

Have you ever used Hilton Honors’ points pooling feature? If so, what was your experience like?

Underdog shows off a 32MB speech model in new demo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

AI companies across the world are racing to make bigger and better models, but the problem is all of them need massive amounts of processing power. On the other end, some researchers and startups are creating smaller models that can run on edge devices, like your phone or computer.

One such startup, Underdog, has released a game built with its new voice model called Woof that’s only 32MB large.

The game features a room with a retro tech theme, a typewriter, and a CRT monitor. There is a desk lamp, which makes a sound when you click its switch. The intro warns users about “intrusive” cloud providers in the town — ostensibly a jab at cloud-based models.

You can interact with the typewriter and music tuner.

We made a game for our new frontier text-to-speech model, Woof. 31.7 MB, so small that it runs in your browser Play the game on X… all voices are Woof. Defeat Darkcloud for an invite https://t.co/ImUFYDCBQG pic.twitter.com/tthHx7ZGBK

You can type anything on the typewriter using your keyboard, and the game will speak it back. You can also rotate the tuner to generate different kinds of music.

All of this works within the browser through Underdog’s speech generation model, which, the company says, can run on most devices without any internet connection. The model has 31 million parameters and is open for everyone to download.

While such a small model likely won’t replace bigger models, it may still be enough to drive local assistant experiences or create personalized podcasts.

This demo comes weeks after Underdog launched Bark, a 167 MB speech-to-text model also designed to run on edge devices for transcription and dictation.

Sigil Wen, the founder of Underdog, told TechCrunch that he started building the app because, instead of using notetakers like Granola, he wanted to build his own system with AI models that can run on users’ devices and let them own their data.

“I think we will be able to run most capable models of today on the devices you already own within months. We just want to train the best open models that are American-made, that are open, that are capable and allow people to have private AI,” he said, referring to his manifesto.

In addition to the two voice models mentioned above, Underdog has also released a 27-billion-parameter model that can run on laptops, along with a waitlisted app that the company is positioning as a locally-hosted alternative to Muse and Instinct.

Thomas Tuchel has urged England fans to end their vendetta against Ben White.

Bayern were ready to pay £58.3m for Rio Ngumoha & may test Liverpool again

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Rio Ngumoha’s future at Liverpool could be under threat next summer.

That’s because Bayern Munich reportedly remain ‘in talks’ with the young Englishman’s camp following a failed push to sign the winger this year.

Christian Falk reports for CF Bayern Insider that FC Bayern were prepared to put their money where their mouth is for the teenager in the summer.

A figure to the tune of €70m [£58.3m] was discussed, however, Liverpool’s uncompromising position on the matter meant that an alternative avenue in Ismael Saibari was instead prioritised.

“It is TRUE: Bayern Munich were prepared to pay €70m [£58.3m] for Rio Ngumoha. As we know, Bayern have been searching really hard for options on the left wing,” the head of sport at BILD said.

“We talked about Bradley Barcola, whom they were in contact with, and they were also in contact with Liverpool’s young talent. There were talks, his brother talked with FC Bayern, but it was a difficult situation for Bayern Munich. It was not clear if Liverpool would sell him or not. The price wouldn’t have been extremely high.

“So, you see, the club’s decision-makers feel he’d be a great option for Bayern Munich, which is why Ngumoha is still on Bayern’s shortlist. In the end, they had to decide for Ismael Saibari. Rio would have been complicated, so they just looked to seal the comparatively easier transfer of the two. Bayern are still in talks with Ngumoha’s camp, however, and he could be an option for the summer.”

You’d have to think Andoni Iraola and Co. will be pretty comfortable despite interest from abroad in Ngumoha’s services.

The player’s move to Anfield from Chelsea – which infuriated decision-makers over in the English capital at the time – was prompted by a desire for a clear pathway to the first team and developmental opportunities.

While the minutes are comparatively fewer and further between in the Premier League, the 18-year-old has been repeatedly trusted in Europe, racking up 285 minutes from a possible 360 in the Champions League. With 628 minutes picked up across all competitions in 2026/27, Rio Ngumoha is already well on course to blow last season’s total (1,065) out of the water.

READ MORE: ‘I like him a lot’ – Romelu Lukaku has been mindblown by Liverpool player he could face tonight

The other thing worth bearing in mind is this isn’t a manager throwing the left-sided winger out on the pitch willy-nilly. Andoni Iraola has shown a clear interest in developing Ngumoha into a more well-rounded, positionally versatile winger. After some initial struggles on the right flank, it’s already paying off.

Yes, at FC Bayern, the former Chelsea academy product would likely get plenty of minutes deputising for Luis Diaz in a less intense league, and the attraction of that possibility can’t be understated.

But if the ultimate goal is development and access to a pathway, he’s clearly already got that at Liverpool.

Sequoia closes its London office, saying it can back Europe from California

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The firm has also shut its Arc accelerator and Scouts programme and merged its teams. Partner Luciana Lixandru turned down a move to San Francisco and is staying in Europe.

Sequoia Capital is closing its London office six years after opening it, and several people will leave the firm. The Silicon Valley investor announced the move on Wednesday in a memo posted on X, titled “The Sequoia of Tomorrow” and signed by its seven general partners.

The firm said the decision was not about the people in London.

“We simply don’t see the need for a local presence. We believe that Europe will continue to produce outlier founders and companies, and that we can continue to provide world class support from our home base in the Bay Area,” Sequoia wrote.

London is one part of a wider overhaul. Sequoia has shut its Arc accelerator and its Scouts programme. It has merged its early-stage and growth teams into one and dropped its Monday partner meeting. The memo also said it had raised about $10bn in new funds. We reported in August that Sequoia was putting $10bn behind AI and reindustrialisation.

The memo said a 20-year expansion across “multiple businesses in multiple geos” had created a hierarchy that “crept over into the investing team”. Sequoia said it had made changes that flatten the partnership.

The London team had four partners: Luciana Lixandru, Julien Bek, Anas Biad and George Robson, Sifted reported. Lixandru joined from Accel in 2020 as Sequoia’s first partner in Europe. She said she was offered a move to San Francisco and turned it down.

“Europe is where my family is, and where I want to build,” Lixandru wrote on X.

She said she remains a Sequoia partner and will keep her board seats, while investing her own money in European founders. Bek is also staying in Europe.

“I’ll continue supporting the founders I work with and serving on my boards. There’s more to build, and I’m looking forward to what comes next,” Bek wrote on X, Sifted reported.

Biad is moving to San Francisco with the firm. He wrote on LinkedIn that he had been spending a lot of time with US founders, and that he would keep meeting founders in Europe. Robson wrote on LinkedIn that his focus was on supporting his founders and a smooth handover, and that he would share his next step later.

The closure reverses what Sequoia said only last year. Lixandru told Sifted then that the firm was set to hire more people in Europe.

Its European bets include Berlin defence company Stark, where Sequoia co-led a €500M round with Founders Fund in June. They also include David Silver’s Ineffable Intelligence. Sifted reported that two of the firm’s San Francisco partners led the Ineffable deal.

Sequoia is not the first big US investor to leave London. Andreessen Horowitz shut its London office last year and Coatue closed its own in 2024, TechCrunch reported. TechCrunch also noted that Stephanie Zhan, an investor since 2015, has left to start her own firm. Jess Lee, who oversaw Arc, is now listed as an operator rather than an investor.

The firm admitted that the changes had a cost.

“It is scary to break something that’s already working just fine, and it is painful to say goodbye to great people,” the memo said.

For ‘Father’s House,’ Former Cult Members and Lauded Actors Collaborated to Explore Mechanisms of Control in a Cinematic Experiment

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Trine Dyrholm, David Dencik, Laura Kronborg Kjær and Emma Sehested Høeg feature in the Tallinn-premiering hybrid doc from a trio of directors.

How’s this for a cinematic experiment? Bring together former cult members and celebrated actors. Ask the performers to recreate events or scenes from life in the sect with guidance and input from the ex-insiders. And then mix reenactments, behind-the-scenes exchanges and former cult members sharing memories and discussing what it was like to face traumatic experiences from the past.

That was the process behind Father’s House, a new envelope-pushing hybrid documentary about the Danish Christian sect Faderhuset, or “Father’s House,” and its 1990s evolution into a tightly controlled community dominated by faith, manipulation and abuse. Related Stories Movies "Saviors of Cinema" and "Money Machine": Tallinn Puts Spotlight on Kids, Youth Films and Social Media Bans Business Dark Slope to Open New Virtual Production Stage in Vancouver (Exclusive)

The movie gets its world premiere on Monday in the Doc@PÖFF International Competition program of the 30th edition of the Tallinn Black Nights Film Festival (PÖFF) in Estonia.

The acting talent helping with the recreation of scenes includes Trine Dyrholm (The Girl With the Needle, Poison), David Dencik (Tinker Tailor Soldier Spy, No Time to Die), Laura Kronborg Kjær (The Sommerdahl Murders, The Girl From Athens) and Emma Sehested Høeg (Killjoy, The Stranger). Dyrholm portrays Ruth Evensen, who is now Ruth Kristiansen, who led the sect throughout the 1990s. It closed as a religious congregation in 2013, with various leaders and members then moving abroad. Dencik plays former sect member Jerry.

The writers and directors behind the project are Adam Nielsen (The Letter Room, The Investigation), Andreas Koefoed (The Lost Leonardo, Music for Black Pigeons) and Eva Mulvad (The Good Life, Enemies of Happiness). Nielsen also edited the film, which features cinematography by Mia Mai Dengsø Graabæk and Adam Jandrup. Father’s House will be released in Danish cinemas via Camera Film on Nov. 19. DR Sales (Mr Nobody Against Putin) is handling sales on the film.

“Moving between high-end dramatic reconstruction and the testimonies of those who escaped, Father’s House explores the fragile boundary between faith and control in an extraordinary world of exorcism, speaking in tongues and militant Christianity,” the PÖFF website says about the doc. “Intimate, deeply unsettling and formally inventive, the film reaches beyond one religious community to examine the mechanisms of manipulation that shape our relationships, our beliefs and the world around us. What makes us believe, obey and need to belong, and how do we recognize when influence becomes control?”

Camilla Johnson, portrayed in the reenactments by Kjær, was a member of Faderhuset for seven years before leaving and setting up the NGO InsideOut, writing books and giving talks to help people leave cults. She and Koefoed talked to THR about how Father’s House came about, how they made sure to avoid triggering former cult members, the reaction they got from the sect’s representatives and why the deep dive into a Danish cult exposes universal mechanisms that audiences will find more timely than they may at first think.

For Camilla Johnson, the film is part of “a journey that started 10 years ago for me,” she tells THR. “When I first started to tell my story, I met Eva Mulvad, one of the film directors. When I wrote my second book, Eva contacted me and asked if I was interested in doing a documentary with her. So we talked about it for a long time. It takes time to build trust and all that.”

After all, “it’s a vulnerable thing” for a former cult member, she shares. “I’ve held many lectures about my life. I’ve been doing podcasts, radio and all that. But it is one thing to talk about it, but another thing to see it. Because when you see things, you feel them in different way.”

Mulvad talked to Nielsen, and they came back to Johnson with an unusual suggestion. “Camilla, could it be an idea that some of you who have been in there actually help direct the actors?” she recalls the proposal. “I was a little bit triggered and said, ‘I’m not sure I can do that because how would that be emotionally?’ Would I be traumatized? I [wondered how it would feel] for me to put the most shameful things in my life out there. Could I handle it? So it was a process.”

But she ended up liking the idea and lauds the film team and the actors for being very respectful, open and thoughtful. “They took something so vulnerable and treated it with respect and care. That has been so big and important,” Johnson emphasizes. “And it was not only the film directors but also the actors who were treating us with real care and respect.”

That helped her get through early caution. “I was very nervous the first time we started shooting. I was very nervous about my own reaction,” she recalls. “But as I was driving home from the set, I felt such freedom. And I felt so grateful for my freedom. It’s been 18 years since I left the cult. I don’t think about it that much anymore because I’m helping other people now.”

And she realized that Father’s House was not really focused on her. “It was never about my story,” she tells THR. “It was about showing the mechanism. So, it’s been a very healing and beautiful process.”

Koefoed joined the film as one of the directors when its concept had already been developed. “I thought it was a really genius concept,” he shares with THR. “I’ve tried before to work with past events and do reenactments and interviews. But there’s always a limit to how close you can get to the truth with reenactments because they are obviously fake. With a fiction film, you get engaged and accept that everything is fiction. But with reenactments, it’s always kind of artificial.”

The process that the Father’s House followed was different. “With this method, you blend documentary and fiction, and you show very clearly what you’re doing when recreating moments and setting up scenes,” he explains.

The approach to the film allowed the former cult members to really become co-creators. “One of the beautiful things in this process has been that we were allowed to be a part of writing the story,” Johnson highlights. “I was a part of choosing the scenes that meant something to me, and we discussed and them in a very close environment.”

Indeed, early in the film, we see Johnson giving notes to Dyrholm, who then asks a follow-up question, creating a real dialogue about how to best portray the cult leader the actress plays.

Narrowing down which experiences and scenes to show in Father’s House was naturally one of the key challenges. “We could have done a whole series, no problem,” shares Johnson.

There is a particularly intense scene, in which Dyrholm as Ruth interacts with Kjær as Camilla in what the latter describes as “the birth scene.” It was an experience that she initially didn’t feel ready to bring to the screen. “When Eva first asked me about that, I said that’s not going to happen because that involved some of the worst abuse I experienced in there,” Johnson recalls. “But then, we talked about it, and as I was processing it, I started feeling that this was an important element. Again, this was not about me. This was about showing the mechanisms of losing yourself to that power when you put God on top of human control. I wanted people to see that and feel that.”

She and the directors hope to give audiences more insight into the cult experience in a way that isn’t condescending. “I think there is a [lack of] real understanding of the people who have been in cults,” Koefoed tells THR. “In society, there is a tendency to look down on them and say, ‘Ah, they are weak, or they are not smart, or whatever.’ What we actually experienced here was that the people in the film are super smart and actually strong. They might have had a vulnerable time in their life where they were seeking a community and answers to some things. But in general, they are just like anybody else.”

He concludes: “We would like the film to achieve lke a better understanding of what it means to be in a cult, why some people enter cults, but also how hard it is to actually leave cults and to get back into society to find yourself again.”

This message and the mechanisms explored in the film also go beyond borders. “Father’s House is a Danish cult, but this is a universal problem,” Johnson emphasizes. “When I traveled to America, when I lived in Jerusalem and Israel, I saw these same mechanisms. Not everybody is going to join a cult, thank God. But a lot of people know how it is to be vulnerable at a time in their life and seek community and fellowship.”

Father’s House feels particularly contemporary and timely, she adds. “We’re living in a time right now where the world is insecure, and that’s why I think this film is super relevant,” Johnson tells THR. “People seek faith. People seek communities. They want to belong. And that’s why I want people to start questioning authority. Because the problem is when you mix religion and control like that, when questioning a human being becomes the same as questioning God, we’ve got a huge problem. And we see that in many places in the world right now.”

So, what do representatives of Father’s House think of the film? “We sent the film to the Father’s House about a month ago, and we gave them two weeks to have a say, to have a comment,” Koefoed says. “They didn’t get back to us, and that was what we had expected.” Concludes Johnson: “That’s how it is.”

John Heitinga was not interviewed for the Hammers job

We subsequently reported details of Parker’s promotion-linked contract arrangements, reflecting the board’s determination to secure an immediate return to the Premier League.

The latest information makes clear that, while other names were considered during the process, Parker was the candidate backed by Virani and unanimously supported by West Ham’s directors.

Houston Center for Contemporary Craft Celebrates 25 Years with Steinbeisser

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Each month, TJ Girard explores the intersection of food and design, from what we eat to how it’s made, presented, and experienced, in her Taste column.

I’ve covered Steinbeisser in this column before, and the Amsterdam studio’s approach to rethinking objects and how we use them continues to hold my interest. Its latest chapter took place on Oct. 23 at the Houston Center for Contemporary Craft (HCCC), where 50 guests sat together at a single long table in the main gallery, surrounded by the exhibition A Material Inheritance, to celebrate the institution’s 25th anniversary.

Houston Center for Contemporary Craft’s 25th-anniversary experimental dinner in collaboration with Steinbeisser. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

Founded in 2009 by Jouw Wijnsma and Martin Kullik, Steinbeisser launched its Experimental Gastronomy initiative in 2012, pairing chefs and artists for one-of-a-kind dinners worldwide. This was its first in Texas and one of the few held in the U.S. The menu was entirely plant-based and seasonal, with produce from regional farms that use organic and biodynamic growing practices. The cooking came from two Michelin-starred restaurants: Tatemó in Houston and Nicosi in San Antonio.

The evening opened with Bohyun Yoon’s Singing Buoys, an interactive work in which guests used handheld remotes to steer blown-glass vessels carrying small bites across a water table. When the vessels collided, they rang like bells.

Guests interact with ‘Singing Buoys,’ an interactive sonic installation by artist Bohyun Yoon, created for the amuse-bouche at the Steinbeisser dinner. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

The dinner itself kept the focus on the objects. Tatemó’s first course of nixtamalized squash, rice, kosho, and carrot came on Jaydan Moore’s Sustenance #1, a platter assembled from salvaged antique silver-plated serving pieces.

The first course featured nixtamalized squash, Koshihikari rice, kosho, and carrot, served on Sustenance #1 by Jaydan Moore, a sculptural platter assembled from reclaimed antique silver-plated servingware. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

Nicosi’s Kudzumochi Tamal, with pumpkin juice, mushroom, masa foam, hoja santa, and gold leaf, was prepared by chefs Jorge Luis Hernández and Ben Hjelm.

Chefs Jorge Luis Hernández and Ben Hjelm of Nicosi plate the second course. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

The course arrived on Adam Knoche’s Exploding Plate, made from crushed basalt clay pressed and fired at 2,250 degrees Fahrenheit to intentionally fracture, alongside his carafe and sauce bowl made from foraged Texas clay and Galveston shells.

CLOCKWISE FROM LEFT: A ceramic carafe by Adam Knoche, made from foraged Texas clays and filled with roasted mushroom broth; a carved maple wood ottchil lacquer spoon by Kyungwon Hwang; a ceramic sauce bowl by Knoche, incorporating Galveston seashells into locally foraged clay; and Knoche’s Exploding Plate. Photography by Kerrisa Treanor; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

Guests ate it with Lisa Marcolongo’s Utensilia spoons, carved from trees felled by storms in Milan and fitted with electroformed recycled copper handles.

A guest uses Lisa Marcolongo’s Utensilia spoon. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

Utensilia spoons by Italian artist Lisa Marcolongo. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

The third course, from Tatemó, featured smoked sweet potato, savory granola, and two moles on Seok-hyeon Yoon’s Zwaart Plate, finished in ottchil, a Korean lacquer made from tree sap and used by monastic artisans for some 2,000 years. It was served with Kyungwon Hwang’s lacquered maple spoons. Nearby, Heejoo Kim’s paper-and-lacquer Gokkal Cups sat in front of Mark Reddy’s towering, carved Standing Spoons.

Gokkal Cups by South Korean artist Heejoo Kim, with Mark Reddy’s monumental hand-carved wooden Standing Spoons. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

Curator Sarah Darro selected the 13 participating artists, and her statement describes how their work turns the table into “a site of experimentation, sensory discovery, and material invention.” Through carving, casting, firing, and weaving, she writes, “familiar utensils become sculptural provocations.”

Guests hold monumental hand-carved spoons by British artist Mark Reddy. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

Guests could purchase the pieces after dinner. Since 2016, Steinbeisser’s online store has offered similar tableware made by artists for its dinners, objects appreciated as much for their beauty as for their thought-provoking functionality.

Ceramic bowls by artist Adam Knoche, crafted from locally foraged Texas clays and finished with richly textured glazes. Photography by Jenny Antill; courtesy of Houston Center for Contemporary Craft.

It was a fitting way for an institution devoted to craft to mark 25 years. The dinner also doubled as a fundraiser for HCCC, and its setting, among an exhibition on silver as both material and metaphor, tied the evening to the institution’s own history. Silver has long been valued for what it reflects, and the dinner offered its own reflection: on an institution’s past and a field that keeps finding new uses for old materials, from storm-felled wood to antique silverware. By handing guests a spoon, a cup, or a plate and asking them to rethink it, HCCC made the case that craft is something to be used, shared, and taken home.

The participating artists, whose work expanded the definition of dining utensils through collaborations with fine-dining chefs, included: Kaye Winwood and Nuala Clooney (glass, USA), Kyungwon Hwang (wood, South Korea), Heejoo Kim (jewelry, South Korea), Adam Knoche (ceramics, USA), Molly Koehn (fiber, USA), Namgwon Lyu (craftsman, South Korea), Lisa Marcolongo (contemporary jewelry, Italy), Edward Lane McCartney (jewelry and multimedia, USA), Jaydan Moore (metalsmith and printmaker, USA), Jakob Niemann (wood, Sweden), Mark Reddy (contemporary maker and artist, UK), Bohyun Yoon (glass, USA), and Seok-hyeon Yoon (designer, Netherlands and South Korea).

TJ Girard is a food designer and creative consultant known for creating theatrical, interactive food and beverage experiences. Based in California, she brings together culinary arts, design, and storytelling to create memorable, multi-sensory events.

You can follow TJ Girard on Instagram, and Linkedin. Read all of TJ Girard's posts.

For its 25th anniversary, Houston Center for Contemporary Craft partnered with Steinbeisser for an experimental dinner where artist-made tableware challenged the conventions of dining

The Tesla Cybertruck Is Dead, And Woke 3 Killed It

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The incredible news just keeps coming after Tuesday's huge victory for everyone not named Elon Musk. After a little more than three years blighting our roads, filling divorce court parking lots, and getting recalled for all of its parts falling off, the Tesla Cybertruck is dead. Never trust Elon Musk further than you can throw him, but early Friday morning, Tesla's CEO tweeted that the company will wind down production of the Cybertruck before finally killing it once and for all at some point next year.

Posting at 12:30 a.m. Texas Freedom Time, a very normal time that every major CEO is awake and making important business announcements, Musk tweeted, "Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production." So there's no word twisting or reading between the lines required. Tesla's Texas Gigafactory will cease production of the Cybertruck next year, and that's the only place Tesla builds the Cybertruck.

Yes, you read that right, folks. The Tesla Cybertruck is dead, and Woke 3 killed it. The Jalopnik staff, Barack Obama, and Karl Marx drove to Elon's house and made him do it. We have that power now. There's lightning in our veins, fire in our eyes, and soon to be a path of destruction in our wake. If we can kill the Tesla Cybertruck, what can't we do? Universal healthcare? Abolishing the electoral college? Free trans for everybody? Finally receiving a sincere apology from my mom? In 2027, we're making it happen.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

It wasn't like it's that crazy for a large, generally established, electric-only automaker to add a pickup truck to its lineup. While a Moscow move may be coming, Tesla's a U.S. company that builds a lot of its vehicles including the Cybertruckkk in the U.S., so Tesla never had to worry about that pesky Chicken Tax. And us U.S. Americans sure love buying our pickup trucks. In fact, it should have been a hit. A U.S.-built pickup truck should have printed money for Tesla, but instead, it's dead after 3 horrible, painful, eye-assaulting years.

But it didn't. After the first blush of sales, interest in the truck fell faster than a stock price after a phony product reveal. The problem was, while looks are subjective, Tesla never bothered to make the Cybertruck good. I used hyperbole when I said all the Cybertruck's parts kept falling off. Technically that isn't true. But it's more true than it should be for a vehicle available for sale to the public, driven on public roads. Tesla's May recall over Cybertruck wheels falling off wasn't even the only recall Tesla had to issue over parts falling off the Cybertruck.

The Cybertruck also famously couldn't survive a car wash, got its butt kicked off-road by a Ford F-150, had still more parts flying off on the road, oh yeah, snapped its frame in an off-road test. You know, good, old-fashioned American truck stuff. The fact that, by the time the Cybertruck launched, Musk had gone mask off as a full-fledged operative for the far-right, spending hundreds of millions of dollars to get Donald Trump elected in exchange for a role gutting regulators, didn't help either. It turns out people who buy electric aren't on Musk's chosen political side.

What's best, from a Republican perspective, is to crush EVs, see them driven before you, and hear the lamentations of the EVs' women. So they aren't what business consultants would call "the most reliable buyers of electric pickup trucks." Throw in prices being far higher after launch than we were originally told, and it shouldn't be surprising that sales were almost as terrible as talking to the owners.

But soon, that will all be over. The Tesla Cybertruck will be no more, and Woke will have won. What a wonderful way to wrap up this already incredible election season. Republicans lost, Elon lost, Texas went blue, the Cybertruck is dead, and America will soon be free once again. God bless America, and God bless these United States.

Pokemon TCG Strife Leads To $300 Queue Holders

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

Pokemon cards are basically impossible to get hold of right now. I am terminally online, have friends who own and work in TCG stores, and regularly frequent supermarkets, and I can barely get hold of them, so I know just how hard it is for the average collector.

The trading card game is now seen as a prime investment opportunity by crypto bros and scalpers, and as such, nefarious techniques, like bots, are deployed to scoop up as much stock as possible. That's also why we see queues forming outside stores 12 hours before they open, since people have no other option unless they want to miss out. Unless...

It turns out, avid Pokemon TCG fans/scalpers/people with too much money are using services and paying hundreds of dollars for people to queue for them to get the hottest products.

The Pokemon Trading Card Game's 30th Anniversary expansion is one of the best in a long time. I called it "the most refreshing in years," and aside from Ascended Heroes, I think it's the best set produced since Evolving Skies. Naturally, such a set has drawn major attention. Lapsed Pokemon TCG fans are returning for the 30th anniversary, long-time fans are, of course, there, and scalpers are seeing it as the perfect chance to make a quick buck—despite tons of stock, competition has never been fiercer.

Things have gotten so bad that people are actually willing to fork out their hard-earned cash, just so they don't have to queue.

Odd-job websites like Airtasker have been flooded with requests from said Pokemon TCG fans/scalpers/people with too much money, asking people to queue for cards.

One listing, which has, unsurprisingly, been filled, is paying $300 for somebody to queue at a Best Buy in California for Pokemon cards. It read, "November 6, there's a Best Buy release of Pokemon cards. Apparently it will start much earlier, so will budget for a 3am start. I can no longer go with my son and I don't want him to miss out. I am open to paying for a couple or two friends to go..."

That's right, somebody has paid $300 on top of RRP just to get their hands on the latest product. At this point, it's almost certainly cheaper to pay scalper prices.

That's far from the only listing, either. A second in California offered $200 for somebody to queue for Best Buy's UPC drop, and multiple other listings for $100+ were made in Texas, Pennsylvania, and Virginia. The world has gone mad.

Right Now Is the Perfect Time to Play the Quietly Best Yakuza Spinoff

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

When Like a Dragon Gaiden: The Man Who Erased His Name was released in 2023, the Yakuza series was in a strange position. The team at Sega's Ryu Ga Gotoku Studio was gearing up to give a heroic sendoff to longtime protagonist Kazuma Kiryu. They had big plans for 2024's Like a Dragon: Infinite Wealth to pass the torch to a new main character. But Yakuza had unexpectedly become bigger than ever outside Japan, thanks largely to the viral success of Yakuza 0 throughout the late 2010s and early 2020s. The fans demanded more Kiryu, not less. And developer Ryu Ga Gotoku Studio rose to the occasion with The Man Who Erased His Name, an interquel that depicts his lonely years in exile after the events of Yakuza 6.

"We could have just told of Kiryu’s past through a thirty-minute interlude as part of Like a Dragon 8 [Infinite Wealth], but we decided it would be a lot more interesting as a game of its own, which is how the project came to be," series producer Masayoshi Yokoyama told Automaton in October 2023. "Though this meant having a whole extra game to make, it still uses the same engine, so we thought 'It’s not like we have to make it from scratch,' and decided to go with it. In the end, it took us about half a year to make it."

While this doesn't sound all that promising, The Man Who Erased His Name delivers some of the series' all-time best storytelling and character work. With Japan's criminal underworld forever changed after a decades-long government conspiracy comes to light inYakuza 6, Kiryu must work with the Daidoji Clan, a shadowy organization of spies and fixers. Our hero knows a bit too much about the connections between the government, police, and organized crime, so the Daidoji threaten his family to ensure he plays along. Armed with identity-concealing sunglasses and magic cigarettes, Kiryu assumes the identity of the secret agent Joryu after faking his own death.

While he's on the clock, Joryu's dishing out pain to dozens of goons at a time. When he's not, Kiryu is adrift and miserable, isolated from everyone he's ever cared about. Yakuza players will be accustomed to seeing Kiryu with the odds stacked against him, but this is a gloomy rock bottom like never before. He spends much of his time meditating in a buddhist temple or smoking countless cigarettes. It's probably best enjoyed if you've played at least one other game in the series, but if you manage to make it through the ending of The Man Who Erased His Name without shedding a tear, you're made of solid stone.

Compared to the mainline Yakuza games, The Man Who Erased His Name is a brief, compact experience — just 15-20 hours compared to the usual 60+. You'll spend most of your time in the familiar Sotenbori district of Osaka, a staple location of the series, as well as the Isezaki Ijincho district of Yokohama from Like a Dragon and Infinite Wealth. The sidequel also adds a new location, the floating vice ship known as the Castle, which features a fighting arena, a cabaret club, Pocket Circuit racing, and the inevitable karaoke.

Best of all, The Man Who Erased His Name is significantly lowered-price on the PlayStation Store right now, discounted from $49.99 to $9.99. The sale runs through Nov. 21. The game is also available on Windows PC and Xbox Series X.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.
