The 9 Most Walkable Cities in the US

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Anyone who’s been to the pump lately could appreciate a little less reliance on cars. And while life in the most walkable cities in the US can mean saving on gas (not to mention car insurance, parking, and potential parking tickets), getting around on foot isn’t only about your budget. Walkability “can benefit everything from household finances and health to the environment and overall quality of life,” says Daryl Fairweather, chief economist at Redfin, which has been following and analyzing walkability for years with their Walk Score tool. “There’s also a housing-market component: People tend to value the convenience and access that comes with living in a walkable neighborhood, and that desirability can translate into high home values. Walkable neighborhoods also create more opportunities for people to interact with local businesses, use public spaces and resources, and connect with their neighbors,” says Fairweather.

To determine the country’s most walkable cities, we’ve reviewed recent studies, like the one from Only in Your State, a travel and discovery platform, plus talked to realtors, residents, and tourism experts across the country.

Hoboken is located on the banks of the Hudson River.

Just across the Hudson River from Manhattan, Hoboken is the most walkable city in the US, according to Only in Your State’s most recent analysis. The one-square-mile-city earned a nearly perfect Walk Score of 97, thanks to its dense street grid, many mixed-use buildings, and abundance of public transportation. Three other cities in the Garden State also cracked the list’s top 10: Union City, West New York, and Jersey City.

Though LA isn’t known for being particularly pedestrian friendly, West Hollywood remains a walkable enclave.

Los Angeles County isn’t known for its public transit, but even so, West Hollywood snagged the fourth spot in Only in Your State’s analysis, earning a Walk Score of 91. The city packs over 300 restaurants, bars, and coffee shops into just under two square miles. West Hollywood’s walkability is especially prized because the area is one of LA’s most vibrant nightlife districts, creating safe accessibility for revelers on the world-famous Sunset Strip.

In addition to being one of the country’s oldest, Boston is also one of the most walkable cities.

“What makes Boston special is that every neighborhood has its own personality and you can experience so much of the city on foot,” says George Sarkis, CEO and cofounder of the Sarkis Team at Douglas Elliman. “You can walk from the historic brownstones of Beacon Hill to the boutiques and restaurants of Back Bay, through the Public Garden, and into the South End without ever feeling like you’re covering much ground.” Walking is the best way to truly enjoy all of the city’s historic sites, centuries-old architecture, and cobblestone streets.

San Francisco’s hilly landscape can turn a casual stroll into an all-in workout.

This Bay Area city has one of the highest transit scores in the country, making it a breeze to go car-free. But for many locals—like Wendy Newman, a realtor with Wesely & Associates and a resident since 1987—walking is the real experience. “​​In one outing, you can buy groceries, stop for coffee, admire a Victorian’s unreasonable paint palette, nod at the neighbor whose face you know but whose name you still haven’t learned, and discover a bookstore you weren’t looking for,” she says. Plus, San Francisco’s mild climate makes taking a spin through neighborhoods like Chinatown and North Beach enjoyable year round.

New York City is built on a grid system, making it easy to navigate.

Just about every expert we talked to mentioned NYC, a metropolis made for a good stroll. From the café-lined lanes of Little Italy to particularly scenic stretches like the East River Promenade and Brooklyn Bridge Park, the Big Apple is big on pedestrian access. And the city’s high walkability score has benefits beyond just saving on cab fare, points out Kate Wollman-Mahan, a real estate agent at Christie’s International Real Estate New York. “Life expectancy in New York City is five years longer than the national average, according to the city-run Health Dashboard and economist Jonathan Miller (82.3 years versus 77.3 years),” she says. “Walking is built into everyday life, from going out to coffee to running errands to walking in the park.”

A stroll around DC can take pedestrians past national monuments and charming townhouses.

According to Walk Score, a research platform that promotes walkability, DC has a walk score of 98. It's such a popular pedestrian playground that even local cultural institutions embed walking into their education approach. Take the Spy Museum, for example, which developed its own free walking tour because of the breadth of strolling space in the area and interest from both residents and tourists. “We send them around the National Mall and downtown area with espionage history that happened in those exact places throughout history,” says Aliza Bran, the museum’s director of media relations. Other particularly walkable neighborhoods include Dupont Circle, U Street, Capitol Hill, and Georgetown.

The Windy City is a certified walking city.

The Windy City tops the US ranking for walkability from Preply, a language learning marketplace that runs walkability studies across the US, Europe, and Asia. “You can walk between Chicago’s five most-visited attractions (the Art Institute, Millennium Park, Cloud Gate, the Chicago Architecture Center, and the Magnificent Mile) in 28 minutes, about 2,500 steps,” says Preply’s communications leader Madeline Enos.

Sheldon Bishop, the founder of BrewAtlas, a guide to independent specialty coffee shops, argues that the best sign of a walkable neighborhood is a good cafe. And Chicago is particularly well caffeinated, thanks to 95 specialty cafes across 41 neighborhoods. “When people can walk to get coffee, they run into neighbors, stop into the bookstore next door, and spend money on the block instead of at a drive-thru,” he says.

Miami is full of walkable pockets, according to local residents.

For Sandra Masis, director of sales at the residential building Arbor Coconut Grove, it’s neighborhoods like Coconut Grove that make Miami such a pedestrian-friendly city. “You can walk to restaurants, cafés, shops, parks, and the waterfront, all while enjoying the neighborhood’s lush greenery and relaxed atmosphere,” she says. “It has a sense of community that makes you want to get outside and explore.” And Miami’s newer developments, like Brickell, prioritize walkability as much as older neighborhoods like Coconut Grove and Little Havana. “Even in a city built around cars, you can create pockets where people can comfortably live, work, and socialize on foot,” says Masis.

Contemporary high-rises mingle with historic sites in Philadelphia.

Every year GuruWalk, a platform for free tours, puts together a ranking of the best cities in the world to explore on foot. In their most recent study, NYC took the top spot among US cities, and Philadelphia came in just behind it. GuruWalk’s Carolina Martínez says that though Philly gets fewer visitors than other places on the list, those who explore it love it. “Philadelphia has a 4.99 average rating, with 99 percent of reviews at five stars,” she says. “Walk a few blocks through the historic district and you’re standing in front of Independence Hall and the Liberty Bell.” New Orleans, San Francisco, and Boston round out the top five.

Hypercall to launch first Anthropic-linked pre-IPO options market

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Trading is expected to begin Monday, with calls, puts and more complex strategies available in select non-US jurisdictions.

Hypercall is launching what it says is the first options market tied to Anthropic ahead of its IPO.

The onchain options platform expects trading to begin Monday, founder Jake Sylvestre and growth and marketing head Dan Edlebeck told The Block. The contracts will trade around the clock but will not be available to U.S. customers.

The options are synthetic, cash-settled derivatives. This means that traders will not own actual Anthropic shares or receive any voting rights when a contract expires. Payouts will be calculated on a contract's strike price and reference price at expiry.

The launch would add options to a market that already offers Anthropic-linked perpetual futures contracts. Sylvestre said Hypercall supports everything from basic calls and puts, which he called "vanillas," to more advanced spreads and strategies. "It's a fully featured options platform," he said. "Any kind of option you want to structure, we can do."

Hypercall is built on Hyperliquid, where existing Anthropic-linked perps give market makers a way to hedge the risk of selling options before the company's shares begin trading publicly. Edleback said that gives Hypercall a head start: "We're able to provide options on these assets before anyone else can," he said.

The platform already offers options tied to SpaceX and the S&P 500 as well as major cryptos like bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) and ether Ethereum (ETH), according to the executives. Sylvestre previously reported that Hypercall handled $536 million in options notional trading volume for September.

The rollout comes as traders become less confident that Anthropic will even go public this month or even before the end of 2026.

On Polymarket, the chances of a November IPO fell from roughly 88% Tuesday to as low as 51% by Thursday, while the probability of a January 2027 debut jumped from less than 1% to nearly 17%. The market had generated around $566,000 in trading volume.

A similar market on Kalshi, with nearly $4.9 million in volume, put the chances of an IPO before Dec. 1 at 60%, down from around 88%. Contracts on a debut before Jan. 1 and Feb. 1 traded at 63% and 85%, respectively.

Technology shares also sold off sharply on Thursday, with the Nasdaq falling around 6% during the session amid growing concerns around AI infrastructure spending.

Sylvestre said the market backdrop had changed his concerns about pricing the options, from a post-IPO rally to a potential slump.

"People that market make options are really pricing volatility," he said. "How fast it drops matters as much as how much it drops."

An option can expire worthless, but a price swing won't automatically trigger a margin liquidation like with trading perps on leverage.

Anthropic filed its S-1 on Oct. 26, with plans to list on the Nasdaq under the ticker ANT. The Claude developer reported $16.3 billion in first-half revenue and $9 billion in compute spending. Its prospectus revealed that its five largest customers accounted for around 25% of revenue through the end of September.

Sony's Killing This Ratchet & Clank Game Before It Even Comes Out

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

One of PlayStation's mobile games is shutting up shop, and it's doing so before it's managed to launch globally.

You may recall Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble, a mobile multiplayer game set within Insomniac Games' beloved sci-fi universe.

It's been quietly making progress with soft launches in specific countries, and was gearing up for a proper, global launch sometime soon. However, developer Oh BiBi has today announced its plans to cease development and close the whole thing down.

The official website for the game has been updated with a notice of the shutdown.

"We've made the difficult decision to cease development of Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble," the statement reads. "The final planned update will be rolling out November 6th, 2026, and the app will be removed from stores on November 21st, 2026."

Players who already have the game can carry on playing through to 1st May 2027, which is the date when the game's servers will permanently go offline. After this point, the game will no longer be playable.

In-app purchases have been removed, and content that was once premium is now available for free, or can be earned through normal play.

The game did release in some countries, including the UK and Canada, as part of a soft launch period before a planned worldwide launch. Back in July, people were invited to pre-register in preparation for the full release, but that will now sadly not be happening.

Ranger Rumble is a competitive multiplayer arena shooter of sorts with cartoonish visuals, various modes to play, and live service elements like time-limited events and seasonal rewards.

On the App Store, the game has a user score of 4.2 (out of 5) after 93 ratings, while it has a 4.4 score after more than 2,000 ratings on Google Play Store.

It's a shame to see the game go down the pan before it really got a proper chance.

Sony does have one or two other mobile games in the pipeline, including Horizon Steel Frontiers, in development at NCSOFT. Hopefully it fares better.

Inside ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’: How an R Rating, Filming in IMAX, and Epic Kaiju Battles Broke Franchise Tradition

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

[Editor’s Note: The following story contains spoilers for “Godzilla Minus Zero.”]

“Godzilla Minus Zero” is gnarlier (it well earns the franchise’s first R-rating) and more cantankerous in its sound design. Still, any fears that the follow-up to 2023’s beloved “Godzilla Minus One” would sacrifice spectacle for sentiment can rest at the bottom of the Pacific Ocean.

Director Takashi Yamazaki returns to direct, write, and aid in the VFX work on his kaiju clobberfest, which is set two years later, focusing on the family comprised of former kamikaze pilot Kōichi Shikishima (Ryunosuke Kamiki), Noriko (Minami Hamabe), and their adopted daughter, Akiko (Sae Nagatani), as they attempt to resume normal life in the aftermath of Godzilla’s attack. While Shikishima’s allies such as Kenji Oda (Hidetaka Yoshioka) have used the tragedy as a springboard to rebuild against all odds, the arrival of a dragon-like beast threatens to dismantle the short-lived peace. Shikishima and his allies try their best to combat the extraterrestrial threat while debating whether Godzilla is worth the risk of resurrection, acknowledging that sometimes the only way to beat one kaiju is with another. Related Stories Rosamund Pike Starring in Body Horror Film ‘Feed’ for ‘Star Wars: The Acolyte’ Creator Leslye Headland Letterboxd Finalizing $330 Million Sale to The New York Times

With this film, Yamazaki burrows into the pointed theme of survivor’s guilt that has characterized his two (and hopefully counting) takes on Godzilla, through new characters like Kanji Murakami (Min Tanaka) and Makoto Aoyama (Masami Nagasawa), exploring how greeting a new day can feel like its own penance. Indeed, how this film plays with Japan’s atomic grievances, tapping into a generation’s latent rage and the notion that opportunity is just tragedy that has shed its skin, feels like a culmination of 30-plus years of storytelling with the character. After watching all the “Godzilla” films this year in anticipation of this one, I can say that “Godzilla Minus Zero” rewards ardent fans of the franchise by building on certain mythologies while cleverly subverting others. Furthermore, it complicates our relationship to the catharsis of a good creature clash by presenting meekness, restraint, and peacekeeping with their own reverential power.

After the movie’s premiere at this year’s New York Film Festival, where the sounds of scorched earth, smelted scrap metal, and kaiju roars rattled in my brain, I tried my best to unravel Yamazaki’s monster mayhem, where, in a spoiler-filled chat, we discussed the influence of “The Thing”, “Intolerance”, and “Close Encounters of the Third Kind”, how the historical fluidity of Godzilla’s greatest foe proved apt for the film’s thematic provocations around adaptation and rebirth, and the creative possibilities that working with IMAX sound and scale afforded him.

The following interview has been edited and condensed for clarity and length. It was conducted via translator.

IndieWire: “Godzilla Minus Zero” breaks the franchise tradition in two key ways: It’s the first Japanese production filmed in IMAX and the first to carry an R rating. How did those production choices help you tell the story the way you wanted?

Takashi Yamazaki: During pre-production and even production, we had conversations with our team that, yes, extreme visual representation or going about the visual expression of the film in the way we were thinking could risk our rating. But I felt, especially for the themes that we were exploring and tackling, to properly communicate that, we could not run away from an R rating. So yes, of course I’d love for kids and families to be able to see this film, but when I weighed everything on a scale between going for the rating and possibly editing or censoring what the message was supposed to be, going all in on conveying this vision won out.

Where we really see the rating apply is in focusing on the bodies that have been charred and incinerated by atomic bombs and Godzilla’s atomic breath.

I could see other films benefiting from more abstracted visual expression or at least visuals that are a bit more sugarcoated, but I did not think that was the case for this film. It was important to show it all in a direct manner.

On the note of IMAX, you used actual aircraft to record engine noises and played monster roars in a mountain dam to capture the acoustics of the kaiju roars. How did the IMAX technology impact your approach to crafting the film’s sonic landscape?

When people hear the term “IMAX,” the focus tends to be on the visual aspects and the imagery. While that’s fair, IMAX’s capacity to express various sounds and create a sonic experience is quite amazing and underrated. When the team and I decided to film “Godzilla Minus Zero” in IMAX, we watched a lot of films to prepare, and no matter the film, I remember walking away from my experiences feeling very shaken to my core. I wanted to evoke that same emotion. When the kaiju roar in this film, I wanted the audience to experience a very real sense of fear. I think back to when we were monkeys and predators would chase after us. That’s the type of fear I wanted people to experience, that sense of being out in the wild and feeling tense because you hear the roar of something that would chase after and eat you.

In a lot of ways, I think the sonic threat is greater than the visual threat back in the day when we would meet predators. When we took the kaiju sounds to record in the dam and hit it with the sound, we wanted to specifically record how those sounds bounced around the environment, into the trees, into the atmosphere. I think what that process did for us is that it took these fantastical creatures and brought them into the real world. Before you see the kaiju, you hear them, not unlike how we might have run from predators when we were monkeys.

What specific animal sounds did you use to craft the roar of the newest kaiju you introduced, Ghidorah?

We looked at various libraries and tapped into some older Ghidorah recordings and then mixed those with recordings of animals such as deer, hawk, tiger, and even some human voices. I wanted to keep the image of the original Ghidorah but add a modern interpretation to it, and something is fitting about Ghidorah’s roar being this amalgamation of all these different creatures. There were various roars we auditioned, but once again, it had to work on an acoustic level, which is where testing the sounds in the dam came in.

You introduce several new characters, and I found myself most compelled by Min Tanaka’s Kanji Murakami. He courses with righteous fury and trauma but does not know how to channel it into anything but revenge. What drew you to craft the emotional spine of this movie around this?

For me, I would say that thematic backbone would be about the twin poles of human foolishness and arrogance. When you look at Murakami’s ideology and actions, I don’t think he thought about what would come after his revenge was actualized. What was on the other side of his actions? What new hate would that create? I think his story, in part, is the story of human history: how acting on hate leads to a chain of events that keeps spiraling onward. On an individual level, we come face to face with a moment where human action can do something, and that was what I wanted to see through his character.

Looking at Godzilla at large and what gave birth to Godzilla, this film is really a reflection of that era. I think at the time of those earlier films, there was a huge fear of what nuclear warfare could mean. People were seriously grappling with this question of “Are we going to go into war again?” That was an anxious tension when Godzilla was born and a byproduct of the sentiment. Those anxieties are very much the fabric of the social context of the people. Exploring Hiroshima was central to “Godzilla Minus Zero” for that reason, but also, in incorporating that history into the main drama of this film, I hope that it hints at how we break the cycle that has defined human history and go in a more hopeful direction.

Focusing a bit more on the kaiju, you previously described making a kaiju film as kagura … “a dance to please and calm the gods, where you place these problems into the shape of a kaiju to help process some of these worries, almost like a ceremony.” You have two “shapes” here with Ghidorah and Godzilla. What were you trying to fill them with?

I think there’s always a dance that happens whenever a “Godzilla” movie is made. It is almost as though the filmmaker and creative team are trying to quell something. The Godzilla movie in and of itself becomes an offering to the gods.

Every time I feel a “Godzilla” movie is released, there’s some sort of imbalance that’s happening in human society that Godzilla is course-correcting. Looking at the two kaiju that are battling it out and sort of what they embody in this film, Godzilla has been spoken about at length in that he originated from this idea and fear of nuclear weapons and the overall terror of war. We have something a bit more abstract in Ghidorah; he is this unknown terror that comes from the sky. So we have, on one hand, war, and on the other hand, terror that comes from the sky. I think the fact that mankind has to rely on war, they have to borrow its power to quell something else, is interesting; it’s almost as if they have to tap into a forbidden realm for salvation given their circumstances.

Ghidorah has always been a kaiju that can adapt — I think of how he becomes Mecha King Ghidorah in Kazuki Ōmori’s 1991 “Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah” — and here, his power set includes assimilating genetic material. It feels fitting given Ghidorah’s powers in past films, but where did this idea originate?

In terms of the look of how Ghidorah “assimilates,” I was quite inspired by John Carpenter’s “The Thing,” where the creature absorbs its surroundings and then turns it into a part of itself. It cannot be divorced from the environment it is in. That feels in line with Ghidorah. For the very first form of Ghidorah as well, I wanted to pay homage to Hedorah, particularly in how it sort of “consumes” and how it moves.

“Godzilla vs. Hedorah” is probably my favorite Shōwa film. Watching Godzilla and Ghidorah fight made me think of Ishirō Honda’s “Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster,” because of the famous fight between those two where they use the environment — such as boulders — to fight. You incorporate Ghidorah’s gravity beam in this film, which can turn a city into ruins and then turn those ruins into weapons. Talk about including the environment more in these fight sequences you crafted.

That’s interesting. Perhaps that final fight could be seen as a reinterpretation for modern audiences of what Honda was doing in his film. Overall, I did feel the idea of the environment itself being a part of the clash or “joining in on the fight” was interesting. When the monsters fight, it’s not in a vacuum; there’s always collateral. For Ghidorah, I talked with the team about how his gravity beam — despite being called that–has not been seen taking advantage of gravity in a way onscreen before. I thought tapping into that would make for a very interesting visual element for these fights.

You’ve also cited Alfonso Cuarón’s “Children of Men” as an inspiration, and the influence shows in the film’s opening with Ghidorah and in the dogfight scene. Why did you feel the language of “long takes” was necessary for these scenes?

These longer shots give a very almost claustrophobic feel where you can’t escape your environment. There was a long take in the middle of Cuarón’s film, where we track a soldier who has to climb upstairs and fight his way up and out of this town that’s become a battlefield. There is no respite in the form of a cut. That had a huge impression on me because through that technique, Cuarón made it so that, as an audience member, even if I wanted to, I could not escape; I could only carry out the mission or die.

In kaiju movies, when there’s a kaiju attack, you can use cuts to either cut to a scene where someone successfully escapes or cut to a scene where they die. There are really only two options. You can create a different dramatic sensation that complicates that binary by having these long takes where these characters are saying, “I have to face this; I have to do something about what’s in front of me because there is no escape.”

Ultimately, I wanted to give the audience this feeling of being unable to escape from a Kaiju, but also because you’re in a movie theater, you also can’t escape from watching that as well. What Cuarón crafted with “Children of Men,” I wanted to create but with a kaiju lens.

To bring up another cinematic reference, you mentioned that D.W. Griffith’s film “Intolerance” inspired you. With this film, you wanted to create a story where “If a small family can attain a small happiness, the world will be OK.”

I believe that the path to happiness around the world is the collective happiness of the individual. “Intolerance” explores this lack of empathy among people. In “Godzilla Minus Zero,” I think this very small happiness in the Shikishima family unit–a unit which is the smallest unit in society–is poignant; despite the tragedies happening around them, if there is even a glimmer of hope and happiness that this very small unit of mankind can bond with each other and be present, perhaps that means all of us can be one step closer to achieving more of the same. So much of this movie deals with “infection,” but in seeing the Shikishima not only cheering that happiness but allowing it to grow even amidst wartime, I think that can be contagious and hopefully spread to their neighbors, and then larger units of society.

GKIDS releases “Godzilla Minus Zero” in theaters on Friday, November 6.

Amorim offers honest Milan reflection: “We’re not ready, we’re far from being a really strong team”

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ruben Amorim continued the criticism of his AC Milan side after their dismal defeat to Ferencvaros, branding them ‘not ready’ to compete on multiple fronts.

Amorim made the decision to enact six changes to the starting line-up from the team that beat Inter convincingly in the derby at the weekend, but it backfired as his team never got going last night and were well-beaten by the Hungarians.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

It is reported that after the game the Portuguese coach gave a long speech to his players about their attitude and effort levels. Milan remain incredibly inconsistent on this front, as they continue to alternate very encouraging performances with disastrous and listless ones.

Amorim laid into his team during an interview with Sky after the final whistle, and then he spoke to the CBS Sports Golazo studio shortly afterwards. The exchange was transcribed by MilanNews.

Yesterday you were talking about identity, about character. Was this the difference?

“Yes, technically, tactically, and above all mentally, they were superior and deserved to win. Today we weren’t ready to play professional football, and I felt it. We need to look within ourselves and do better.”

And in the Champions League the level would be even higher…

“We have to focus on the small details, without thinking about the Champions League or anything like that. We just have to focus on improving the more specific aspects of football: the mentality, the heart, the passion. We have to improve on that.”

What is Ferencvaros’ level in European football, how do you rate them?

“I don’t know, it depends on the opponent, but they’re doing very well. They have four wins in four games, a clear identity, and today they were superior to us.”

What did this match tell you about Milan?

“That we’re not ready. We’re not ready, we’re far from being a really strong team. We have moments, in some games, where we’re really strong, but then we can’t maintain our level, especially mentally. So this game told me and all of us that we’re not ready to be a very strong team.”

During the first half, I noticed you were very energetic on the sidelines, and then it almost seemed like you stopped giving directions. Can you tell there’s a certain lack of understanding within the team and that they’re not playing the way you’d like?

“No, I just do what I feel at the time, and sometimes I don’t do certain things just for show. We didn’t play well, that’s for sure; we had a completely different attitude in the last league game, while today we weren’t mentally prepared to fight for the win, and that’s what we need to work on.”

If there hadn’t been the derby at the weekend, do you think this match would have gone better?

“I have no idea, it doesn’t matter. It doesn’t matter, we have to be ready to play every three days in different competitions. So it doesn’t matter if we had a match before or not. I think we have to improve a lot in all aspects of the game.”

Torriani played his first match as a starter. How do you rate his performance today?

“I think he did well in the first half. He had a difficult time with a pass, but Lorenzo doesn’t bear any responsibility for the team’s performance. In fact, in the first half he gave us time to try to make some changes in the second half. So Lorenzo just has to keep working. We have to improve as a team to be stronger.”

How Remedy designed Control Resonant’s wildest mission: ‘Anything kind of goes’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Time-travel stories are ‘illogical’ by nature. But for Resonant’s version to work, Remedy had to make the physical space a lot easier to follow.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Time-travel stories are ‘illogical’ by nature. But for Resonant’s version to work, Remedy had to make the physical space a lot easier to follow.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Note: Some spoilers for Control Resonant’s “Recursions and Iterations” mission follow.

One of Control Resonant’s most memorable missions involves time travel — kind of. There are certainly multiple timelines intersecting, or maybe overlapping, or maybe running in parallel. As protagonist Dylan Faden, you meet some of the researchers who are trapped in and investigating these timeline anomalies. Dylan’s first encounter with the researchers is somehow not the researchers’ first time meeting Dylan. But before we can learn much more, time glitches out, and they all turn up dead.

That seems to be the end result in every timeline Dylan comes across, even toward the end of the mission. What does it all mean for this timeline, the one we’re playing? Did the act of tracking down the researchers change our timeline? Are we in a different timeline entirely?

I wasn’t sure what to make of it when I finished the mission, and I’m not alone — even the developers at Remedy had trouble keeping track of it all. “I don’t actually remember where we landed on with the writing,” says lead level designer Anne-Marie Grönroos, turning the question over to senior level designer Admir Burnic. “Like, are the researchers even there after the mission is done? Are they still living their life?”

Burnic isn’t sure, and I tell them I’m not entirely sure, either. But that’s not really the point of the mission. Like many of Remedy’s best sequences, the “Recursions and Iterations” mission is more about the unease of knowing something is wrong but not exactly what it is, the subtle horror of realizing generally what’s been happening but not necessarily why. Maybe the narrative team did establish a canon ending for the researchers and all three of us just missed it, or maybe it was ambiguous on purpose.

“It’s funny, when you’re dealing with this sort of time-travel stuff, timelines, whatever, there’s always this aspect to it where it’s almost illogical,” Burnic says. “So anything kind of goes. I mean, it could be intentional. It could also not be intentional.”

Toward the end of “Recursions and Iterations,” there’s a mind-bending combat sequence in which a bunch of Dylans, presumably from the other timelines, glitch in to join the fight. Burnic says this was a somewhat late addition inspired by “Effect and Cause,” a mission from Titanfall 2.

There’s this time-travel mission in Titanfall 2. I was really inspired by that. I wanted to do something along those lines, but I was looking for “What’s the Remedy version of it?” And at some point I had this idea of glitches of Dylan showing up executing enemies. I remember it was fairly late in production, so it was a bit risky. I knew I can’t just go and pitch this. I have other stuff to do at the same time. So I was finding something that was relatively easy to do, a way to reuse Dylan’s animations that we already have. So I remember just going to one of our animators and going, “Hey, can you just throw me some of these animations? I have an idea.”

Either way, the storyline was a consistent standout for Remedy’s playtesters, Grönroos and Burnic tell me. What they had to fine-tune — and very intentionally so — was the language and narrative of the physical space. In the final version of the mission, Dylan has to travel between three different research stations in the labyrinthine Underpass, an expanse of floating concrete islands, to search for the researchers. Ambiguity here was going to be a problem.

“In the beginning, it was, like, identical stations, and we would more rely on the narrative content inside of stations to [tell you] what happened there,” Burnic says. In the final version, the stations share the same layout but are in visibly different states of disarray and disrepair. “We found a sort of language we could use. Station A was, ‘They were here for two weeks,’ station B [was], ‘They were here for three months,’ and I think the last one was the extreme one, which was like, ‘Oh, this was 10 years into this.’”

The team used that concept as a guide to visually differentiate each station from the others. “And that came very much from a need,” Grönroos explains. “When we had people playtesting that mission before, they didn’t even know how many stations there were. Like, there were people who thought there’s one station, maybe there’s six stations, who knows, because they look so identical and the environment is not that easy to tell apart, either.”

There were, aptly, a lot of iterations of this, and art was just part of it. One of the biggest changes was mechanical: You need the Reach ability, which allows Dylan to essentially grapple over long distances, to access the Underpass area to begin with. This wasn’t an explicit requirement early on, but it makes the floating islands of the Underpass much easier to navigate. And “it was the navigation issues that were keeping this mission down” during development, Grönroos says.

But Dylan’s abilities presented their own problems. “If I remember correctly, we wanted to have certain beats, certain narrative moments, being represented to you first, and I think one of the biggest difficulties for me especially was that Dylan as a character is so powerful. The gaps he can close with his powers is quite insane, right? And it was so easy to break [the flow],” Burnic says.

“I remember at some point kind of realizing that, however you twist and turn it, you can never really guarantee that a player sees this, and trying to guarantee it will be a bad design because it will be too hand-holdy,” he adds. “It was more like these narrative moments were pieces of the puzzle, almost. So it doesn’t matter which one you get to first, but hopefully the player will be able to put them together once they have all the pieces.”

With a supremely powerful character like Dylan, Burnic says, “you need to lean into it and just design in a way where you can trust the player.” And with multiple timelines involved, seeing certain things out of the intended order wasn’t going to break the overall story of the mission.

I certainly got turned around a few times in the Underpass and doubled back to look for collectibles I might’ve missed. But I didn’t struggle too much, and I understood the story Remedy was trying to tell — maybe not every single detail possible, but the most important beats, and definitely the unsettling, otherworldly mood it created.

That was ultimately the “fragile balance” they had to strike, Grönroos says: trying “to have enough guidance that you understand what you are doing and where you’re supposed to be going” without holding the player’s hand.

“It’s in the DNA of the whole game, really.”

Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 review: The new standard for smartwatch battery life

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Coupled with a bright display, it hits the sweet spot for smartwatch hardware.

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Samsung's latest Ultra smartwatch hits all the notes of an 'ultra' wearable: a solidly designed timepiece with chunky detailing that puts activity tracking front and center. For the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 ($700), the company has made major improvements to battery longevity and performance, while still forging its own health-tracking path with a bunch of unique metrics (of varying utility). With 13 years of wearable launches behind it, can Samsung pry me away from my Apple Watch Ultra?

It's refreshing to see some major hardware upgrades on this second Galaxy Watch Ultra — perhaps a benefit from taking a year off from offering an upgrade. Starting with the (slightly bigger) 1.52-inch display, it now reaches up to 5,000 nits of brightness, a big leap from the original Watch Ultra's 3,500-nit max — and better than pretty much every other smartwatch out there. It's the same squircle design that most folks either love or loathe. I like it: It looks different from the circular smartwatches and won't be confused for an Apple Watch either.

Speaking of which, Samsung's newest flagship watch is lighter and slimmer than both its predecessor and Apple's latest Watch Ultra 4, but it's still a substantial timepiece, at 10.7mm thick and weighing just shy of 2.2 ounces (61.5 grams). It comes in only one size, 47mm, and it looks chunky and outdoorsy on most folks' wrists. If you're looking for a more subtle, smaller Samsung smartwatch, the company's Watch 9 series might be a better fit, literally. The company has stuck with its proprietary Dynamic Lug System for its watch bands, so you'll be limited to Samsung-made options and a few niche third-party options. on't expect the same range you get with Apple's wearables.

There's still no rotating bezel, which was a mainstay on Samsung's older flagship wearables, and the buttons along the right edge protrude much less, which is one of several small hardware refinements you may not notice at first. Sadly, Samsung also reduced the orange detailing on the main button, which I liked. I'll take anything that adds a splash of color to monochromatic tech.

The lack of a physical dial doesn't ruin the wearable, but it can be frustrating at times. Using picker wheels, which you scroll to choose a number, seem overly sensitive across multiple apps. For example, when adjusting the weight numbers on Hevy, a weight training app that works on the Watch Ultra 2, I found it hard to lock on to the right number.

The hardware seems as durable as it looks, with a shock-resistant titanium casing and IP69K dust and water protection, making it completely dust-proof and able to handle high-pressure, high-temperature water jets. This is arguably the most diving-friendly mainstream wearable, or at least it will be. It has a water resistance rating of 10 ATM, so that you can take the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 as deep as 100 meters (or 328 feet). However, we're still waiting, months after it was announced, for a dedicated diving app that was promised at launch.

Battery capacity is up substantially, from the original Watch Ultra's 590mAh cell to 800mAh. That's over a third more battery. Combined with the latest Snapdragon Wear Elite chipset, it delivers the battery life I want from a smartwatch.

We all use wearables differently: some want constant heart rate tracking and some want fine-grained fitness tracking during daily workouts. Some might want their wearable to track their entire ultramarathon, and the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 will oblige. I am not an ultramarathon runner — yet. With mid-to-light use and the always-on display on, I got three days out of the device before the battery dipped below 20 percent. This included plenty of notifications, tracking an hour-long gym workout most days and using the Watch Ultra 2 controls to play podcasts and music. I also used it to track my sleep each night which I'll dive into more later.

Already, that's impressive battery performance. Over a week, I'd typically charge the device only twice. When I headed out for a weekend break, my fully charged Watch Ultra 2 handily lasted the trip. Skipping tracked workouts or turning off the always-on display, I got closer to four or five days of use. I want this to be the new standard in smartwatch battery life, though I understand that, at least for now, bigger batteries demand the thicker design of these 'ultra' smartwatches.

Coming from over a decade of Apple Watch ownership, interspersed with various Samsung wearables (running different operating systems), I still find it hard to navigate to the apps and status screens I want. However, it's still leagues ahead of the dark Tizen years.

It feels like too many inputs. Alongside the three hardware buttons on the right side, navigation centers on swiping from the top edge, while swiping from the bottom edge and side edges takes you elsewhere. Swipe down opens the Watch Ultra 2's Control Center (brightness, settings and volume), while swiping up opens the app drawer. Swipe right to get to your notifications, though it's a bit odd that they aren't in the swipe-down menu, given everyone's muscle memory. Meanwhile, swiping left drops you into a mix of tracked fitness metrics and weather.

Speaking of the app drawer, I think the Watch Ultra 2's app icons are often too plain and similar-looking, making it hard to find what I need at a glance. Having to depend on the alphabetical list (and scrolling and scrolling and scrolling) is rough. I rarely use Samsung's home-built Android apps, preferring either Google or third-party options, so I haven't built up a memory of most of the app icons. Samsung offers an app panel with your six most recently used Watch apps, which can even include the settings menu or Google Play Store, helping reduce my struggles with the wearable's app list.

Samsung split some of its health features between two apps: Health and Health Monitor. The company did so to keep medical-grade features like ECG (electrocardiogram) and sleep apnoea detection separate, and to obey regulatory compliance and medical device approvals in different regions. Samsung can then roll out (or limit) clinical features by location based on that criteria. Still, finding the health tracking features I wanted was often laborious, with half the features (like sleep metrics) requiring me to open my smartphone to see them.

There are highlights too. Samsung has built a playful range of watch faces and wallpapers, mixing chronograph-style, information-heavy options with customizable, photo-centric portraits and brightly colored numeric options. Uploading your own photos to the watch remains oddly sluggish, however.

Samsung goes to town on tracking biometrics, yet again. Antioxidant levels, hydration tracking and body composition scanning are here, on top of deeper sleep and workout tracking. Loud noise detection is new for Samsung's wearables, too, a feature that's been on Apple's Watch series for a few years.

Some new features are hidden away inside the Watch Ultra 2 (or more often than not, your companion Samsung smartphone), but they're aimed at better synthesizing all this data into a more palatable, understandable form. For example, a new feature called Heart Health Score attempts to distill multiple tracked metrics into a single number, combining sleep, stress and activity and connecting it to body composition data.

It's a clear example of how many wearable features and settings get buried, and how easy they are to miss. To see your score, you have to open the Heart health tile on your watch, scroll down and tap "Show on phone" to launch its own Health app on your paired device. I wish this (like many other metrics) could just be displayed on the Watch Ultra 2 itself.

We've already tested out the new hydration tracking during exercise, which seems aimed at more intensive, lengthier workouts than I usually do. It could be a useful addition for hardcore athletes, but for the rest of us who work out in the gym or train for a half-marathon at most, hydration isn't a major issue. One quick aside: The Galaxy Watch Ultra 2 automatically detects workouts and walks well, flagging sessions more consistently than my Apple Watch Ultra 3.

Once again, we tested the antioxidant tracking, which requires you to take off the Watch Ultra 2 and press your thumb firmly against the sensors on the rear of the device. For a week, I tried to lean into the healthy eating habits Samsung says will increase my antioxidant levels. It's a lot of common-sense suggestions: Eliminate added sugars, avoid ultra-processed snacks and meals, increase raw veggies and fruits. Interestingly, Samsung's app suggests leaning into moist-heat cooking — like steaming, instead of grilling or roasting.

While we saw antioxidant testing in last year's Samsung wearables, the Watch Ultra 2 adds two features: trend charts and daily history logs. My antioxidant levels increased towards the upper limit of low, but never quite graduated to the middle rating. I'm not sure it's a particularly notable way to track healthier eating, but it kept me honest in my food choices for a few weeks.

Sleep tracking has been upgraded, although the new software finally landed months after the Watch Ultra 2 initially went on sale. There are some genuine improvements here, including a faster setup. The watch now requires only three days of sleep data from the past two weeks, instead of the seven days previous Galaxy Watches needed.

Despite its stellar battery life, the Galaxy Watch suffers from the fact that most of us recharge our smartwatches overnight — or prefer not to sleep with a slab of tech attached to our wrist. Folks who charge their watch overnight can't gather sleep data, so needing fewer days of data makes it easier to get on board with sleep tracking.

There's also a sleep coaching 'pet', based on an initial setup survey and your first few nights of sleep pattern data. This offers sleep advice and habit suggestions, setting up a 4- to 8-week coaching program that adjusts dynamically based on the sleep you eventually get.

Samsung has offered snore detection on its wearables for years, which still requires your phone nearby to record audio and it's still harrowing to listen back to. With the Watch Ultra 2, Samsung upgraded sleep apnea detection and folded it into its Health Monitor app. It requires two nights' sleep, with the watch worn tightly and higher up the wrist. It'll then let you know if you show signs of sleep apnea, which can be a genuine health threat.

The watch monitors biometric readings throughout the night, including blood oxygen levels, heart rate variability and your physical movement. It tracks signs of moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) and analyzes breathing disruptions per hour. It's not a clinical diagnosis, but it could be worth getting an expert opinion if the watch detects high levels of sleep interruption. If you're not using a Galaxy phone with this watch, unfortunately, you won't be able to use several features, including this sleep apnoea test. Other features unavailable include the ECG, which checks for signs of atrial fibrillation, and the blood pressure monitor.

Samsung's refinements to its Galaxy Watch Ultra series prove it can match Apple's equivalent Watch Ultra. The hardware is solid and premium, with its own unique style, while the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2's battery life blows away most of its smartwatch competition — a crucial, if not the most important, tech spec in a wearable. The display is also incredibly bright, too, making it easy to use on sunny days, and the always-on mode doesn't demolish the battery life like the feature often does on older wearables.

Samsung continues to test multiple health metrics and lifestyle tracking options with mixed results. Sleep tracking has improved and needs less data, so I didn't feel forced to wear the watch every night. But other features, like antioxidant and hydration tracking, can be inconsistent or even irrelevant.

At a time of rocketing tech prices, Samsung's top-tier smartwatch is $100 cheaper than the Apple Watch Ultra 4. Still, because they're on separate platforms, that decision hinges less on which watch is better and more on whether you use an iPhone or a Galaxy device. The bigger competition may be Samsung's Galaxy Watch 9 ($380), which packs the same array of deep health monitoring features and AI shortcuts for several hundred dollars less. The Galaxy Watch 9 lacks the Watch Ultra 2's standout battery life and bright display, though, both of which make this Samsung's most capable wearable yet.

Physicl raises $10M to make the digital world real enough for robots

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Alex de Vigan’s last business got swamped by the rise of generative AI, but now he’s betting the tech that team had built, a platform for creating tempting product renderings for e-commerce, can be re-purposed to make robots more useful.

His new venture, Physicl, has raised $10 million in seed funding from Inovia Capital to help world model designers and robot builders solve thorny training problems with more realistic simulations.

Founder and CEO de Vigan spent the last decade building nfinite, which made it easy for brands and retailers to create 3D models of their goods and display them online. The arrival of transformer-based AI models led that company to focus more on generative imagery, but de Vigan saw a new purpose for his team and the tech stack they’d built to faithfully render 3D environments.

“What we were super unique about doing in 3D, AI was going to replace, but something was missing in AI: its ability to understand, interact, and manipulate space and objects,” de Vigan says.

Researchers developing physical AI models to make robots more capable of tasks like handling diverse, small objects have been frustrated by the returns of training them on video, and the expense of gathering data through remote operations.

Simulations in digital worlds are seen as a promising solution, but ensuring that these simulated worlds are faithful to real-world physics has proven difficult. de Vigan believes that the pipeline his previous company built to “transform unstructured, incomplete, heterogeneous real-world information into a high-fidelity physical representation… with correct geometry, materials, and physics” can now be used to make physical AI models more effective.

Physicl is entering a crowded category of startups vying to provide data and tooling to foundation model builders and robot operators. “The space is very young and very blurry sometimes,” de Vigan admits. There are a variety of companies collecting, labeling, or generating data in different modalities as their customers try to figure out the secret sauce that will make robots live up to their promise.

In particular, Phyiscl wants to focus on post-training models on difficult edge cases. The startup is releasing a product called Studio with 10,000 3D environments and 100,000 assets that represent various objects. It lets users create scenes to generate synthetic data for training robots on specific problems. For example, Studio could be used to generate training data to help a robot that has trouble discerning a cup on a cluttered counter-top, or while the cup is in shadow.

SpAItial, a world model builder, collaborated with Physicl as an early customer to improve the fidelity of its products, the latest of which, Echo-3, was released last week. SpAItial’s tool generates 3D simulations from 2D imagery, and to do that effectively, its CEO and founder Matthias Niessner said they needed to train on the kind of high-fidelity synthetic data Physicl offers.

However, Physicl wants to be more than a data provider; it sees itself as the tooling for ongoing synthetic data generation. “What we created 10 years ago, without knowing it, was basically an agentic workflow to transform unstructured information into high fidelity 3D,” de Vigan said. He imagines customers bringing trillions of images or millions of hours of LIDAR capture data to feed into his platform and turn them into high-quality 3D representations that can be used to generate billions of permutations to train their models.

“The real challenge is, you have to curate the data by hand, and this is a very long cycle,” Niessner, also a professor of robotics at the Technical University of Munich, told TechCrunch. “To us what seems very attractive is you can just do this on the fly […] You’re running Claude, and it checks, ‘Oh, I need more data of this in my training,’ and then it auto generates that data, depending on the training state. That, to me, is super exciting, and this is exactly what Physicl is offering.”

Deus Ex Creator Warren Spector Tears BioWare-Closing EA Executives A New One

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Warren Spector, the developer famous for working on some of the greatest games of all time, is mad. Really, really mad. Like so many others, he’s been hit hard by the news of EA’s closing BioWare, and two weeks later he’s still reeling. Now he’s calling for senior heads to roll in an industry that only ever seems to punish the creatives they stifle.

Two weeks ago, as news broke about the closure of BioWare by the now-Saudi-owned EA, the usually cordial Spector posted on his LinkedIn that he was driven to cursing by the news, describing the “devastating historical event” as “utter bullshit.” He noted that he’d like to see the “folks in suits” losing their jobs, asking them to consider, “what do you contribute to gaming? What price do you pay for failure?” and declaring his wish to see those people on the unemployment line, not the developers who “do the real work.”

Over the last fortnight Spector’s mood has only worsened. Noting that he’s very aware of the scale of layoffs across the whole games industry, he explains that “for some reason, BioWare hit me on an emotional level that nothing had before.” He adds, “I felt gut-punched.”

There were those who, after the news broke that the studio behind Baldur’s Gate, Dragon Age,Â andÂ Mass Effect would be no more, argued that the studio’s best days were already clearly behind it, and that it made business sense to close what was left of the once huge studio given the commercial failure of Dragon Age: The Veilguard. “Fair enough,” says Spector, “but those folks clearly didn’t read my whole screed. Near the end I expressed that I was incensed that the business people who make the decisions that lead to studio closuresâALL of them, not just BioWareânever seem to pay a price.”

Going on to call such people “culture-killers,” Spector continues, “If studio-level people leave, resulting in dilution of talent, someone is creating an environment that pushes them out.” And he points out that somehow those executives rarely seem to be the ones paying the price. “Do they lose sleep let alone jobs?” The furious tirade continues,

Do those same executives suffer like developers when the needs of financial partners and private investors take precedence over the people who actually create value, let alone over the interests of players? Does anyone up there in or adjacent to the C-suite care?

Is there ANY accountability left in this world?

Obviously not. At EA alone Andrew Wilson still makes his $38.65 million; Laura Miele, $13.7 million; Stuart Canfield, $11.3 million; Jake Schatz, $8.4 million; Mala Singh, $8.3 million.

Meanwhile, Spector points to the tens of thousands of developers who are out of work as a result of poor management. “And these people think they’re worth that kind of compensation?”

The legendary creator finishes his post saying, “And if you think this is a discussion about ‘sound business decisions’ I hope you never get anywhere near running a business. Or at least that people have the sense not to go work for you.”

Stevie Nicks Predicted the Midterm Blue Wave in a Moving Poem: ‘The Light Creates a Circle of Angels’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Fleetwood Mac singer said she wrote "Morning Symphony" three weeks before Tuesday's massive rebuke of the Trump agenda.

While many Americans watched Tuesday’s midterm election results with either great anticipation or resigned dread, Stevie Nicks was not sweating the final tally at all because she already knew how the night would end.

The Fleetwood Mac singer and solo star revealed in a post on X that she wrote a poem on Oct. 18 as a kind of wish into the universe. “It was sort of like a little prayer for the coming elections,” she said. “I really believed that what has happened was going to happen; it just had to. Good almost wins … I didn’t have time to get it out before the election — so here it is. That’s the dream …”

The dream came true, as Nicks — who endorsed former Democratic Vice President Kamala Harris in the 2024 election — and the rest of the nation watched the electorate soundly rebuke the second Trump term agenda by giving Democrats a historic blue wave victory that has swept them into the majority in both the House and Senate, in addition to flipping five governorships and racking up massive wins on the local, state and national level.

In the poem titled “Morning Symphony,” Nicks muses on the beauty of early mornings and her dog Lily, her sadness about what is going on in the country, ending with a final, flickering hope that the light will win out over darkness.

I get up and walk to the window and I don’t sleep —

Back in August, Nicks, 78, made a surprise appearance at Olivia Rodrigo’s Daisy Chain Fields festival where they performed the Fleetwood Mac classic “Landslide.” The two women also contributed to the soundtrack of Practical Magic 2, which featured “Landslide” and Rodrigo’s “The Cure.”

How to find Ice Caves biome in Minecraft

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There are loads of unique biomes you can find and explore in Minecraft, with plenty of fresh ones being introduced throughout updates. One of the newer additions is the Ice Caves biome, which means you may need some help finding it.

With lots of regions to explore and a consistently expanding number of biomes you may encounter, tracking down a specific one on your own can be tricky. Venturing to them all is key for discovering new items and checking off important tasks, though, so here’s how to find the Ice Caves biome in Minecraft.

The Ice Caves biome is a cave region that spawns at lower temperatures. This means you’ll most commonly find them situated underneath snowy biomes such as Ice Spikes, Snowy Plains, Snowy Taigas, and Frozen Oceans. Sometimes, though, they can also appear under biomes that are less cold, like the Dappled Forest.

Ice Caves are also overall more common near oceans, so look out for cold regions situated near the ocean to have the best shot at finding them. High humidity levels reduce the likelihood of Ice Caves spawning, so don’t search for them around biomes that have this feature, like Jungles.

Since Ice Caves weren’t always a feature in Minecraft, finding them on older worlds is tough. If you want to locate one in a world you had prior to the Winter 2026 drop, you’ll need to venture to an area you haven’t been to before, as they cannot spawn in chunks you’ve already loaded in. Because of this, it’s best to create a fresh world if you’re hoping to find this region.

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

If you want to guarantee you successfully locate an Ice Caves biome, there is a special cheat you can use to do so. The following command can be entered in the chat box with cheats enabled to give you the coordinates of the closest Ice Caves biome.

Once you’ve entered this cheat, you can click on the coordinates in the chat box to teleport to the biome. Or, if you wish to travel on foot, you can also navigate to it yourself using these numbers.

Ice Caves are a cold biome where lots of different icy blocks can generate. Blocks you can find while exploring this area include calcite, packed ice, icicles, ice crystals, snow, brittle ice, and ice. Ice crystals and icicles spawn exclusively in this area.

Frostbites, an icy variant of the Zombie, can spawn here. You may also encounter other common cave hostile mobs, like Spiders, Skeletons, Slimes, Endermen, and Creepers, while exploring this area.

Once you’re done exploring this special region, you can move on to tackling other key Minecraft tasks. If you’re unsure what to do next, consider getting a straw bed, learning about all Sulfur Cube archetypes and block absorption effects, finding the Bounce music disc, and making a geyser.

Mortgage Calculator: Here’s How Much You Need To Buy a $430K Home at a 7.18% Rate

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates retreated to a new one-month low this week as bond yields softened in the wake of the historic midterm elections in which Democrats reclaimed control of the House and Senate.

The average rate on 30-year fixed home loans drifted down to 7.18% for the week ending Nov. 5, down 6 basis points from 7.24% the prior week, according to Freddie Mac. This marks the third consecutive week of easing borrowing costs, providing ongoing relief after rates touched three-year highs last month.

Even with this retreat, borrowing costs remain higher than they were a year ago, when rates averaged 6.22% during the same period in 2025.

So what does this mean for homebuyers? Using the Realtor.com® mortgage calculator, we can look at how the math works out for the median-priced home in the U.S.

All examples assume a 30-year fixed mortgage and include principal and interest only, excluding property taxes, homeowners insurance, and mortgage insurance.

For a homebuyer eyeing the median house price of $430,000, a 20% down payment results in a loan amount of $344,000.

At today's 7.18% rate, the monthly principal and interest payment is approximately $2,330. This reflects a $14 monthly reduction from the previous week’s payment of $2,344.

Compared to the 6.22% average from November 2025, which required a $2,111 monthly payment for a home at this price, today’s buyers are paying $219 more every single month.

The monthly costs have also risen markedly for those using FHA loans with a 3.5% down payment.

On a $430,000 home, an FHA borrower would finance roughly $414,950. At today’s 7.18% rate, the monthly principal and interest payment comes to approximately $2,811.

This reflects a $17 decrease from last week's monthly cost of $2,828. Compared to the 6.22% rates of November 2025, where the monthly payment for this loan amount sat at $2,547, today’s FHA borrowers are paying an extra $264 in interest every month.

However, looking back at the October 2023 peak of 7.79%, where the payment for a home at this price reached $2,984, today's monthly payment still offers $173 in relative relief.

The long-term financial picture shows how this continued downward drift improves overall borrowing costs over a 30-year term.

A buyer with a 20% down payment at today’s 7.18% rate will pay a total of $838,935 in principal and interest over the life of the mortgage.

While current financing costs remain above year-ago levels, this total still represents a distinct contrast to the October 2023 peak of 7.79%, when the total cost for that same $344,000 loan would have reached $890,630. By securing a mortgage at today’s rate instead of that peak, a homebuyer effectively avoids $51,695 in interest charges over the 30-year loan life.

FHA borrowers see a similar trajectory of long-term figures. Financing the current median-priced home at today's 7.18% rate results in a lifetime payment of $1,011,966 for principal and interest.

If that same loan had been locked in at the 7.79% peak in late 2023, the total cost would have climbed to $1,074,323. This represents a total long-term savings of $62,357 for FHA buyers.

While the gap above 2025 rates continues to weigh on overall affordability, the steady decline over the past three weeks is helping to restore some predictability and savings for active fall buyers.

Dina Sartore-Bodo is the senior editor at Realtor.com covering real estate news, personal finance trends, and interior design. She previously served as the managing editor at HollywoodLife.com, the executive editor at PerezHilton.com, and as a managing editor at Mediavine. Her work has also appeared on MSN, Yahoo News, and BlogHer. She is a proud graduate of Emerson College in Boston and is originally from New Jersey.

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Thomas Tuchel has left Cole Palmer out his England squad while Jobe Bellingham, the brother of Jude Bellingham, has received his first senior call-up.

Tuchel was critical of Palmer for withdrawing from September’s squad due to injury, with the England manager saying the Chelsea star missed “too many camps with injuries”. Palmer did not miss any games for Chelsea following the international break, but was sent off against Tottenham, and the 24-year-old has now been left out for the Nations League games against Croatia and Spain as the Three Lions aim to book their place in the quarter-finals.

Speaking at a press conference at Wembley, Tuchel explained: “It counts when you are in camp and I just said it last time, he [Palmer] misses too many opportunities to impress. It was a long camp. It was two-and-a-half weeks and the players and the boys who were in camp built something extraordinary throughout these weeks. That's why the credit goes to them. The trust is for them. If you are in camp, this is your chance to impress. So it's not personal.”

Elsewhere, Jobe Bellingham, 21, joins brother Jude in Tuchel’s squad after impressing for Borussia Dortmund this season, while there is also a first-time call-up for Fulham midfielder Josh King, 19. Trent Alexander-Arnold keeps his place despite his lack of minutes for Real Madrid while Arsenal right back Ben White also returns following injury, but there is no place for winger Noni Madueke despite the absence of Bukayo Saka due to injury.

England are third in Group A3 after four games, having won three and lost one, to Spain at Wembley. They host Croatia on Thursday before travelling to Madrid to take on Spain on Sunday, and a point from either of those two games would be enough to qualify for the knockout rounds in March.

Follow all the latest updates from England’s squad announcement and Tuchel’s Wembley press conference below.

From childhood Champions League finals on a patch of grass in Stourbridge to the biggest stage in international football, Jobe Bellingham is determined to step out of his brother’s shadow.

Myles Lewis-Skelly is absent for England’s match against Croatia due to suspension.

Thomas Tuchel addressed this saying: “We have a slight issue in central midfield because Alex Scott is not ready. Unfortunately he picked up an injury in the last camp and can’t be with us.

“Myles, who featured in all four matches, picked up a yellow card and is suspended for the Croatia match and it leaves us a little bit short in central midfield, especially for the first match.

“(Under-21s boss) Lee Carsley has spoken highly of Jobe for weeks and months that if there is a spot and possibility to join and make an impression in the first team.

“He had good matches, and he is on a good run of form in Dortmund, so he deserves to be with us.”

Jobe Bellingham and Josh King have been included in the final England squad of the year, but Thomas Tuchel has overlooked Cole Palmer and Kobbie Mainoo for November’s internationals.

Djed Spence and Ben White were brought into a 25-man squad that defender Nico O’Reilly has dropped out of.

Declan Rice returns after injury but Bukayo Saka is absent with a thigh injury and Tuchel decided not to bring in Noni Madueke as his Arsenal team-mate’s replacement.

There was no place for World Cup squad members Dean Henderson, Dan Burn and Jordan Henderson after missing the last camp.

Alex Scott, Marcus Rashford and John Stones are ruled out of this meet up through injury, as are Tino Livramento, Reece James and Trevoh Chalobah.

England will play the final two matches of their Nations League group stage in this break.

First up is Croatia on Thursday 12 November before a blockbuster clash against Spain on Sunday November 15.

The England boss ended his press conference by answering a question on right-back Trent Alexander-Arnold.

He said: "He was very good in the last camp and in the games he played, and he deserved to come back.

"The competition is on, as always. The door is always open. He competes with Jarell [Quansah] and Ben [White] for the right-back position."

Thomas Tuchel was asked about the inclusion of younger players such as Jobe Bellingham and Josh King and whether that signals a new phase for the team.

"I think we did naturally. We were a little bit forced. I was a bit concerned before the last camp because we missed Dean Henderson, John Stones, Dan Burn, Jordan Henderson, Reece James - players who were really responsible for the attitude and the energy at the World Cup and played a huge part in the standards throughout the camp,” Tuchel explained.

"I was a bit worried that we missed so many of them but the group found a new way. They found new leadership, a new way to engage with each other. We built another camp with a good atmosphere, new voices to set the standards.

"Now we have more choices and I had to give Dan Burn, Jordan Henderson, Dean Henderson difficult phonecalls. I am very aware of what they did for us and they built a very strong foundation on which these players now are benefitting from.

"Naturally, we went forward. But we always want to go forward and embrace change as something positive. It does not mean the door is closed for these kinds of players. But we decided to give this group kind of a second push."

The England manager has opted to ‘trust’ the players who impressed him in the last squad, while also offering first-time call-ups to Josh King and Jobe Bellingham.

On Jobe Bellingham’s call up, Tuchel said: "It's an incredible achievement for the brothers and the family to be both nominated for the national team, which is an incredible story in itself.

“I was very happy to give him the call-up and I saw his excitement straight away in his smile. He is a very open and calm personality so it was a very nice phone call, of course, because it was very good news.

"He is one of five players for the central midfield. He is a more defensive player than his brother Jude. Good on the ball, good physique, very physical player who can keep possession. Now it is up to him to impress."

“Me personally, I’m targeting top spot and I would be very surprised if my group doesn’t.

“This is clearly the target, we want to have a final in Spain because we want to have these pressure moments and these matches when we play for something.

“We want to play under pressure and in the big occasions. We want to use the Nations League for exactly that to be prepared in the best possible way for the Euros.

“For me, Croatia is a crucial match to win especially after the result we got. We got a deserved result [7-0 in Croatia] but it was kind of a freak result that does not reflect the quality of Croatia in general.

“It reflected the way the match went last time but we have to forget that result and remember what we put in and what quality we put in and what effort we top.

“We need another top performance because Croatia showed an excellent response against Spain straight after.”

"I have little a standby list and the clubs and the players know it, players who cover us on a lot of positions,” Tuchel explained.

"But we have 22 outfield players so this should not affect us. It is a short camp and we should be fine number-wise.

"It is always about the ones who are in camp, who don't withdraw and walk the talk, come to camp and show that it matters.

"I think that [message] is naturally coming through. That was always the message very, very early since we took charge that is matters if you are in camp and what you do in camp.

"The last camp was an excellent one and the trust and belief is in the players who were there."

Liebherr expands excavator range with new, 250-tonne electric model

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It takes some seriously heavy equipment to make an ultra-class mining truck look little, but that’s exactly what German equipment giant Liebherr does best, and its newest, 250-tonne production excavator keeps that tradition alive – only this time, it’s electric.

Liebherr began converting its diesel-powered mining excavators to electric in limited numbers back in 2024, and electric power has proven to be effective – moving millions of tons of dirt while saving millions of dollars in fuel and maintenance costs. Now, the company is translating that testing experience into regular production practice with the launch of a new, all-electric machine. Meet the R 9300 G8-E.

Based on the proven R 9300 G8 platform, the new Liebherr R 9300 G8-E packs an 850 kW (~1,140 hp) electric drivetrain designed to IEC standards and supports 6-6.6 kV power supplies. The company says its electric setup offers customers the same hydraulic power and bucket capacity (1.8 tonnes/cubic meter, or ~1.5 short tons/cubic yard) as the diesel version.

All that electric power ships with advanced electronics and operator assistance systems designed to maximize both efficiency and safety while giving mine operators more data to work and plan with. Liebherr describes its take on ADAS thusly:

The R 9300 G8-E comes with Liebherr Power Efficiency – Hydraulic Control as standard, which adapts hydraulic power to suit the machine’s load profile, reducing overall engine load factor. Thanks to its Generation 8 electronic architecture, the R 9300 G8-E is compatible with a range of Liebherr’s IoMine technology products, including its range of excavator Assistance Systems, the semi-autonomous Bucket Filling Assistant, the Skyview vision system as well as the data visualisation tool, Data View. Customers also have the option of installing Liebherr’s autonomous cable reel solution onto the excavator to assist with cable management and support machine mobility on site.

Like its repowered cousins, the big electric excavator does seem to have on-board batteries, but is primarily driven and operated with grid power, reducing charging and re-charging concerns and enabling round-the-clock operations for as long as grid power holds.

Liebherr touts the benefits of new R 9300 G8-E’s quiet, emissions-free running throughout its official announcement, but doesn’t stop there. The company says its EV, which makes do with thousands of fewer moving parts in its drivetrain, can also reduce maintenance requirements and associated operating costs in certain applications and site conditions while improving workplace conditions for the people who work in and around the machine.

The electric drivetrain also reduces vibration inside the cab, improving operator comfort, limiting fatigue, and potentially contributing to a safer working environment.
